# 40 | Desire, Demand, Fantasy, Graph, and Che vuoi?

## Source and edition note

This file is a cross-source analytic synthesis. It is not a transcription of a single work and it must not be read as though its prose were Jacques Lacan's. Claims about Lacan are anchored primarily in the project corpus's primary Lacan sources: the Bruce Fink complete English translation of the *Écrits*; Russell Grigg's English edition of Seminar V, *Formations of the Unconscious*; the available project translation of Seminar VI, *Desire and Its Interpretation*; the official English edition of Seminar X, *Anxiety*; and Alan Sheridan's English translation of Seminar XI, *The Four Fundamental Concepts of Psychoanalysis*. Freud is used only where his concepts supply a genuine genealogical background to Lacan's argument. [Source note: project corpus architecture; Lacan primary sources listed above.]

The principal *Écrits* anchor is "The Subversion of the Subject and the Dialectic of Desire in the Freudian Unconscious," especially the graph sequence on English pages 681, 684, 690, and 692 and the ensuing discussion through page 695. The argument about demand, love, need, and desire is also controlled by "The Direction of the Treatment and the Principles of Its Power" and "The Signification of the Phallus." [Sources: Lacan, *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject," pp. 681-695; "Direction of the Treatment," especially the later sections on demand and desire; "The Signification of the Phallus," especially pp. 579-581 in the Fink edition.]

Seminar V is used as a developmental source rather than treated as if it simply contained the final graph in finished form. It gives the 1957-1958 construction through fantasy, the formulas of desire, the distinction between desire and demand, the clinical study of the neuroses, and the later appendix that reproduces the complete graph while explicitly referring readers back to *Écrits* pages 681, 684, 690, and 692 for its staged development. [Source: Lacan, Seminar V, chs. XIII, XVII, XIX-XXVI; Appendix A, "The Graph of Desire," p. 485 ff. in the English edition.]

Seminar VI is used as the major 1958-1959 laboratory of desire and fantasy. The available project text preserves session dates and source-page markers but is not treated as having the same authorised English-edition status as Fink's *Écrits* or the official English Seminar X. Where its wording is important, the formulation is identified by session and source marker rather than silently harmonised with later published terminology. [Source: Lacan, Seminar VI, sessions beginning 12 November 1958 and 19 November 1958, and the later Hamlet and fantasy sequence in 1959; project translation status retained.]

Seminar X is the decisive chronological pivot for object *a* in this file. Earlier texts already position fantasy, loss, the object, and the Other's desire in ways that prepare the concept, but the mature formulation of object *a* as the **cause of desire**, lying "behind" desire rather than serving as its ordinary intentional target, is treated as a later development and not projected backwards without qualification. [Source: Lacan, Seminar X, ch. VIII, "The Cause of Desire," source pp. 100-104; later 1963 sessions on anxiety, object *a*, and the desire of the Other.]

Seminar XI is used for the 1964 reformulation of the subject-Other relation through alienation, aphanisis, and separation. These operations do not erase the earlier graph. They rearticulate how the subject comes into being in the field of the signifier and how the subject responds to the lack encountered in the Other's discourse. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XI, chs. 16-20, especially "The Subject and the Other: Alienation" and "The Subject and the Other: Aphanisis," pp. 203-229, and the later return to the desire of the Other.]

Bruce Fink, Alfredo Eidelsztein, and *A Compendium of Lacanian Terms* are used as interpreters and technical guides. Their contributions are explicitly marked as interpretations. Their systematisations are not allowed to overwrite Lacan's own chronology or to turn a difficult formula into a settled textbook slogan. [Interpretive sources: Fink, *The Lacanian Subject*, especially chs. 5-7; Eidelsztein, *The Graph of Desire*, especially chs. 3-10 and 13; *A Compendium of Lacanian Terms*, entries "Desire" and "Object a".]

## Scope and method

The organising problem is the sequence **need → demand → desire → desire of the Other → `Che vuoi?` → fantasy → object *a*** and the way that sequence is formalised by the graph of desire, then reworked through anxiety and alienation/separation. The file distinguishes conceptual levels that are often collapsed in simplified accounts: a biological or vital requirement is not yet a demand; a demand cannot be reduced to the satisfaction it explicitly requests; desire is not simply a hidden item demanded more intensely; the Other is not merely another person; fantasy is not ordinary daydreaming; object *a* is not the empirical object somebody wants; and anxiety is not defined here as a fear with no object whatsoever. [Sources: Lacan, *Écrits*, "Signification of the Phallus," pp. 579-581; "Subversion of the Subject," pp. 681-695; Seminar X, source pp. 100-104 and later lessons.]

The method is chronological and comparative. When the same term carries different theoretical weight in 1957-1958, 1958-1959, 1960, 1962-1963, and 1964, the differences are retained. The graph is treated stage by stage, with its visible labels and directional relations checked against rendered source pages. The final synthesis therefore does not assume that Lacan had one completely static system from the outset. [Sources: Lacan, Seminar V, Appendix A; Lacan, *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject," graph sequence; Seminar X; Seminar XI.]

Throughout the file, source labels such as `[Source: ...]`, `[Sources: ...]`, and `[Interpretation: ...]` are internal research provenance markers. They are designed to survive plain-text transfer into a notebook environment. Quotations are kept short because the purpose here is synthesis and navigation rather than reproduction of the underlying copyrighted works.

## Coverage of this file

The body proceeds from the need-demand-desire distinction through the formula "desire is the desire of the Other," the `Che vuoi?` question, the staged graph of desire, the structure and matheme of fantasy, object *a* as cause of desire, lack in the Other, Seminar X's theory of anxiety, Seminar XI's alienation and separation, and the distinct ways hysteria and obsession organise desire where the primary sources warrant such distinctions. It then compares the developmental sequence across Lacan, identifies disagreements among interpreters, and ends with common misreadings and an integrated concept map.


## 1. Orientation: The Problem of Desire in Lacan

Desire is central in Lacan because it names neither a simple biological push nor an inner preference waiting to be discovered by introspection. It appears where the living being has already been taken up in language, where a request must pass through the Other, and where satisfaction therefore cannot be reduced to the delivery of an object. The decisive terrain is not a psychology of preferences but a structure in which signifiers, demand, the Other, lack, fantasy, and the subject's division condition what can be wanted at all. [Sources: Lacan, *Écrits*, "The Signification of the Phallus," especially pp. 579-581; "Subversion of the Subject," pp. 681-690.]

A concise formula such as "desire is the desire of the Other" can therefore mislead if detached from the sequence that gives it sense. In the late 1950s Lacan is simultaneously developing the distinction between need, demand, and desire; the dependence of speech on the locus of the Other; the retroactive production of signification; the graph of desire; the subject's relation to fantasy; and the clinical differences between ways of sustaining desire. The formula does not mean merely that people imitate what other people want, nor simply that everyone wants approval. It is a proposition about how desire is constituted in the field of the Other and about how the subject confronts an opacity in the Other's desire. [Sources: Lacan, Seminar V, especially the later clinical section on desire and demand; Seminar VI, 12 and 19 November 1958; *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject," p. 690.]

The chronology matters. Seminar V, delivered in 1957-1958, supplies the first major construction site. Its work on wit, metaphor, paternal metaphor, the phallus, fantasy, and the clinic of demand/desire progressively produces the schema later known as the graph of desire. The English edition's Appendix A explicitly states that the definitive graph is the one reproduced in the *Écrits* on page 692 and that it was preceded by versions found on pages 681, 684, and 690. The appendix thus itself presents the graph as an elaborated sequence rather than an isolated diagram. [Source: Lacan, Seminar V, Appendix A, "The Graph of Desire," English p. 485 ff.; visual inspection of project PDF physical p. 487.]

Seminar VI, 1958-1959, then turns desire itself into the seminar's explicit problem. Lacan tells his audience that the graph had been built progressively the previous year around the formations of the unconscious and that its usefulness would become clearer as the seminar articulated desire. Early in the year, `Che vuoi?` is introduced in connection with the Other's answer to the subject's appeal and with the fact that the subject does not transparently know what he is doing in speaking. The seminar's dream readings and the long Hamlet sequence then make fantasy, the Other's desire, interpretation, and the timing of action central. [Source: Lacan, Seminar VI, 12 November 1958 and 19 November 1958; later 1959 Hamlet sequence in the project transcription.]

In 1960, "The Subversion of the Subject and the Dialectic of Desire in the Freudian Unconscious" compresses and formalises much of this work. Lacan says there that the graph had been constructed in his seminar on formations of the unconscious, beginning with the structure of jokes. He then gives a staged sequence: an elementary cell, a second graph identifying the Other and punctuation, a third graph organised by the `Che vuoi?` question, and the complete graph with an upper and lower level, fantasy, drive, lack in the Other, jouissance, and castration. This text is the cleanest primary-source map of the graph's internal articulation and is therefore the central technical anchor of this synthesis. [Source: Lacan, *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject," pp. 671-702, especially 681, 684, 690, 692-694.]

Seminar X, 1962-1963, does not merely repeat this framework. It reopens `Che vuoi?` through the more pointed `Que me veut-Il?`, exploiting the French ambiguity to ask not only what the Other wants *with* me but what the Other wants concerning the place I occupy. Most importantly for the later history of the object, Lacan explicitly argues that object *a* is not the intentional object located in front of desire. It is to be conceived as its cause, and he says that the object lies "behind" desire. Anxiety, correspondingly, is said not to be without an object, although the relevant object cannot be identified in the ordinary way one identifies an empirical object of fear. [Source: Lacan, Seminar X, opening lessons on `Che vuoi?`; ch. VIII, "The Cause of Desire," source pp. 100-104; later lessons on anxiety.]

Seminar XI, in 1964, makes another shift. The key operators become alienation and separation in the field of the Other. The subject is produced as an effect of signifiers through an alienating choice, while separation concerns the encounter with a lack in the Other's discourse and the enigma of the Other's desire. Lacan again uses the losange that appears in the fantasy formula and in the relation of the subject to demand/drive, but the explanatory apparatus is now explicitly logical. The earlier graph remains relevant, but the subject-Other relation is no longer presented only through the graph's staged architecture. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XI, chs. 16-20, especially pp. 203-229 and 244-276 in the Sheridan edition.]

These developments make two methodological cautions essential. First, later terms should not be retrojected as though they had always possessed their later sense. Seminar V can be read retrospectively as preparing object *a*, but the mature formulation of *a* as cause of desire belongs centrally to Seminar X. Second, a later interpreter may make the sequence easier to teach by presenting it as a unified system, but that pedagogical clarity is itself an interpretation. Fink's account of alienation, separation, and traversing fantasy, and Eidelsztein's technical reconstruction of the graph, are valuable precisely when their status as reconstructions is visible. [Interpretations: Fink, *The Lacanian Subject*, chs. 5-7; Eidelsztein, *The Graph of Desire*, chs. 3-10.]

The wager of this file is therefore not to produce a single slogan for desire but to reconstruct a controlled chain of problems. A vital need must be articulated; articulation turns it into demand; demand addresses an Other and carries a dimension not exhausted by the requested object; desire emerges in the gap opened by this transformation; the Other's own desire is encountered as opaque; `Che vuoi?` condenses that opacity into a question; fantasy supplies a structure in which the barred subject relates to an object; Seminar X reframes that object as cause; and Seminar XI shows how the subject's constitution and separation depend on lack in the field of the Other. [Sources: Lacan, *Écrits*, "Signification of the Phallus," pp. 579-581; "Subversion of the Subject," pp. 681-695; Seminar X, ch. VIII; Seminar XI, chs. 16-17.]


## 2. Need, Demand, and Desire

### 2.1 Why the distinction is structural

The distinction among need, demand, and desire is not a three-part vocabulary for different degrees of wanting. Lacan uses it to mark what happens when a living requirement is taken into language. A need can be described at the level of an organism: hunger, bodily comfort, or another requirement may be satisfied by some specific provision. But once the need has to be articulated to another through a signifying system, it does not arrive unchanged. The request is addressed, heard, interpreted, delayed, refused, anticipated, or answered by an Other whose response has value beyond the object delivered. [Sources: Lacan, *Écrits*, "The Signification of the Phallus," pp. 579-581; Seminar V, later lessons on desire and demand.]

This is why Lacan refuses to derive desire from a simple biological deficit. In "Subversion of the Subject" he says that desire's irreducibility to demand is also what prevents desire from being reduced to need. The graph is introduced precisely to locate desire in relation to a subject constituted through signifiers. The question is not how a natural appetite acquires a mental representation, but how the signifying mediation of need transforms the coordinates within which satisfaction can occur. [Source: Lacan, *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject," p. 681.]

### 2.2 Need enters the defiles of the signifier

Lacan's phrase that demand passes through the "defiles of the signifier" is central. The subject's dependence is not simply the infant's physical helplessness. Physical dependence is sustained within a universe of language. Caregivers interpret cries and gestures; they give names and rhythms to what is happening; and the subject's needs become differentiated within that field. By the time the need returns as a human demand, it is already marked by the Other's code and by the contingency of the Other's response. [Source: Lacan, *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject," pp. 687-689.]

Seminar VI makes the same point developmentally without reducing it to a developmental psychology. Lacan says that even before a child can sustain discourse, the mark placed on need by demand can already operate through alternating cries. At a further level, the child encounters the other not merely as the supplier of an item but as a presence capable of answering an appeal. The fort-da scene matters here because presence and absence themselves acquire signifying value. [Source: Lacan, Seminar VI, 12 November 1958, project source marker around p. 10.]

Freud's fort-da observation supplies a background for this problem but not Lacan's complete solution. In *Beyond the Pleasure Principle*, Freud describes the child's game in which disappearance and return are repeated. Lacan takes such material into a broader theory in which absence/presence is not merely represented but becomes part of the signifying articulation of demand. [Background: Freud, *Beyond the Pleasure Principle*, fort-da discussion; Lacan, Seminar VI, 12 November 1958.]

### 2.3 Demand asks for more than its stated object

Demand has a double dimension. It can request something determinate, yet the fact that it is addressed to the Other introduces a demand for the Other's presence, recognition, or love that is not identical with the specific satisfaction asked for. "The Signification of the Phallus" makes this especially explicit. Lacan argues that demand bears on something other than the satisfactions it calls for and that what can be granted is transmuted into proof of love. The concrete satisfaction therefore cannot settle the whole demand. [Source: Lacan, *Écrits*, "The Signification of the Phallus," pp. 579-581.]

The phrase "demand for love" does not mean that every request secretly asks for romance or affection in an ordinary psychological sense. "Love" here names the value conferred by the Other's response beyond the usable object. A glass of water, food, reassurance, information, or a particular act can be requested as a finite thing, but the response may also be read as a sign of what the demander is for the Other. That second dimension is structurally open-ended because no finite object can finally establish the value of the subject for the Other. [Sources: Lacan, *Écrits*, "Signification of the Phallus," pp. 579-581; "Direction of the Treatment," later sections on demand.]

This gives demand an unconditional edge. The specific need has conditions under which it can be satisfied, but the demand for the Other's presence or love seeks something no particular object can guarantee. Lacan's point is not that subjects consciously formulate an infinite demand. It is that the signifying address changes the function of what is given. A satisfaction can become evidence, or fail to become evidence, of the Other's response. [Source: Lacan, *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject," pp. 688-690.]

### 2.4 Desire as the remainder or gap

The often-cited formula that desire results from the subtraction of need from demand is useful only if it is not treated as literal arithmetic. In "The Signification of the Phallus," Lacan says desire is neither the appetite for satisfaction nor the demand for love but the difference that results from their splitting. Desire therefore occupies the gap produced when need has been articulated as demand but the dimension opened by that articulation cannot be closed by satisfying the need. [Source: Lacan, *Écrits*, "The Signification of the Phallus," pp. 580-581.]

"Subversion of the Subject" states the same logic spatially: desire begins to take shape in the margin in which demand tears away from need. This margin is produced because the appeal to the Other cannot be reduced to a universally specifiable satisfaction. Desire is thus not a mysterious surplus added to a fully satisfiable need. It is an effect of the transformation itself, of the gap between what can be supplied and what the signifying address has opened. [Source: Lacan, *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject," p. 689.]

This is also why desire is "eccentric" to satisfaction in Seminar V. If desire were simply the name of the object required to end a state of tension, adequate gratification would extinguish it. But Lacan is interested precisely in the persistence, displacement, and renewal of desire across satisfactions. The late Seminar V section repeatedly differentiates desire and demand because a treatment oriented only by frustration and gratification risks mistaking the analytic problem for the management of supplies. [Source: Lacan, Seminar V, ch. XIX, "Signifier, Bar and Phallus," beginning around English p. 315.]

### 2.5 Desire is articulated but not transparently articulable

Lacan's 1960 formulation that desire is "articulated" but not therefore "articulable" guards against another simplification. Desire is articulated because it is carried by signifiers, substitutions, displacements, repetitions, and the structures in which the subject speaks. But it is not articulable as a final, exhaustive statement of the form "what I really want is X." The signifying chain can produce formations in which desire appears without yielding a metalanguage in which the subject finally says what desire is once and for all. [Source: Lacan, *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject," p. 681.]

The distinction is clinically decisive. If an analyst treats the explicit request as the transparent truth of desire, analysis collapses into advice, gratification, or negotiation. If the analyst simply frustrates every demand in the hope that desire will automatically emerge, the result is equally mechanical. Lacan's point in "Direction of the Treatment" is that demand and desire must be distinguished in the conduct of treatment, not that one should moralistically oppose them. [Source: Lacan, *Écrits*, "The Direction of the Treatment and the Principles of Its Power," especially the sections on demand, desire, and interpretation.]

### 2.6 Freud's Wunsch and Lacan's désir

Freud's theory of dreams is an indispensable background because it places wish (*Wunsch*) and its distorted fulfilment at the centre of dream-work. The supplied early English *Dream Psychology* translation also foregrounds condensation and displacement as mechanisms through which dream-thoughts are transformed. Lacan explicitly re-reads these Freudian mechanisms through metaphor and metonymy. Nevertheless, Freud's *Wunsch* should not simply be translated conceptually into Lacan's *désir*. The Compendium is useful here precisely because it warns that the Freudian wish and Lacanian desire do not exactly coincide. [Background: Freud, *Dream Psychology*, sections on wish-fulfilment, condensation, and displacement; Interpretation: *A Compendium of Lacanian Terms*, "Desire," p. 55 ff.]

Freud also distinguishes sexual aim and sexual object, showing that drive cannot be understood by assuming a fixed natural pairing between impulse and object. That background makes it possible to see why Lacan can later distinguish the object that appears to be wanted from the object that causes desire. But Lacan's object *a* is not already contained as such in Freud's early sexual theory; the relationship is genealogical, not identity. [Background: Freud, *Three Contributions to the Theory of Sex*, discussion of sexual aim and sexual object; Lacan, Seminar X, ch. VIII.]

### 2.7 The minimum conceptual sequence

The safest compressed sequence is therefore:

1. **Need** designates a vital requirement considered before its full signifying transformation.
2. **Demand** is need addressed through language to the Other, acquiring a dimension of presence/absence and love beyond the particular requested satisfaction.
3. **Desire** emerges in the split opened by that transformation. It is not identical with the requested object and is not extinguished by ordinary satisfaction.
4. Because demand is addressed to the Other, desire cannot be understood without the field of the Other and the question of what the Other wants.

[Sources: Lacan, *Écrits*, "The Signification of the Phallus," pp. 579-581; "Subversion of the Subject," pp. 687-690; Seminar VI, 12 November 1958.]

This sequence should not be reified into chronological stages that one simply completes in childhood. The terms are structural distinctions. In adult speech a request can still carry need, demand for love, and desire in different articulations at once. The graph's usefulness lies precisely in representing crossings and retroactions rather than a one-way developmental staircase. [Sources: Lacan, *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject," Graphs 1-3; Interpretation: Eidelsztein, *The Graph of Desire*, chs. 3-4.]


## 3. Desire as Desire of the Other

### 3.1 A formula with more than one register

"Man's desire is the Other's desire" is one of Lacan's most compressed formulas and one of the easiest to flatten. In "Subversion of the Subject," Lacan himself pauses over the grammar. He distinguishes an objective use of the French *de* from a subjective determination and says that, in the latter sense, it is "qua Other" that man desires. The grammatical clarification is evidence that the formula is not intended as a single everyday proposition such as "I want whatever someone else wants." [Source: Lacan, *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject," p. 690.]

At least four closely related dimensions need to be held apart. Desire is mediated by the symbolic field of the Other; the subject's desire is formed through signifiers that are not privately invented. Desire also takes shape in relation to the Other's desire, which can appear enigmatic. Desire can seek recognition from the Other, a Hegelian register Lacan uses but also revises. Finally, the subject may desire from the position of the Other, so that the question is not simply which object belongs to "me" but from where and according to what coordinates the object becomes desirable. [Sources: Lacan, Seminar VI, 12 November 1958; *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject," pp. 689-691; Seminar XI, later field-of-the-Other lessons.]

### 3.2 The Other is not just another person

The capitalised Other, `A` in Lacan's notation, is first of all a locus. In Graph 2 of "Subversion of the Subject," `A` is the site or treasure trove of signifiers; `s(A)` marks punctuation or the point at which signification is produced. The Other may be embodied by a parent, lover, analyst, institution, or another concrete person, but it is not reducible to that individual's psychology. The distinction matters because "desire of the Other" is not equivalent to "guess what this other person privately wants." [Source: Lacan, *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject," pp. 682-684, Graph 2 on p. 684.]

Seminar VI makes this concrete. The child encounters an other who can answer an appeal and whose response determines whether particular signifiers appear within the exchange. The experience of the Other's desire therefore concerns the subject's dependence on a locus from which meanings, prohibitions, names, and responses come, not merely the emotional mood of one caregiver. Lacan's developmental examples stage a structural relation. [Source: Lacan, Seminar VI, 12 November 1958, source marker p. 10.]

### 3.3 The opacity of the Other's desire

If the Other were only a stable code translating needs into satisfactions, there would be no `Che vuoi?` problem. But the Other is encountered as desiring. Seminar VI describes the Other's desire as opaque or obscure and links that opacity to the subject's helplessness. The subject must locate its own desire within a space already opened by the Other's desire, yet the Other does not provide a transparent final answer about what the subject is for it. [Source: Lacan, Seminar VI, early sessions, especially the sequence following 12 and 19 November 1958.]

This does not mean that the Other possesses a hidden, fully formed intention that the subject has failed to decode. The later doctrine of lack in the Other blocks that conclusion. The Other itself lacks a final signifier capable of guaranteeing the whole system. Thus the enigma of the Other's desire is not solved by finding a secret message behind appearances. The Other is structurally unable to furnish the complete guarantee the subject seeks. [Source: Lacan, *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject," pp. 693-695, `S(Ⱥ)` and "there is no Other of the Other." ]

### 3.4 Recognition is part of the story, not the whole story

Lacan's engagement with Hegel makes recognition unavoidable. Desire can be bound to the desire to be recognised and to the prestige of being the object of another's desire. But "Subversion of the Subject" also criticises the Hegelian master-slave scenario and uses the obsessional to expose the fantasy of waiting for the master's death while keeping one's own desire at a safe distance. Desire of the Other cannot therefore be reduced to a benign desire for mutual recognition. [Source: Lacan, *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject," pp. 685-687.]

Seminar X sharpens the divergence from Hegel. Lacan explicitly contrasts the relation of desire to the desire of the Other with the Hegelian schema of competing self-consciousnesses. At the level of object *a*, the subject cannot find the cause of desire simply in itself. This gives the formula a dimension that recognition theory alone cannot contain. [Source: Lacan, Seminar X, later lessons on obsessional desire and the desire of the Other, source pp. 329-332.]

### 3.5 Desire from the place of the Other

The phrase "qua Other that man desires" shifts attention from the object to the position from which desire is articulated. The subject's signifiers, ideals, prohibitions, categories of desirability, and even the question by which a situation is framed come from a field that precedes conscious self-definition. To say that desire is mediated by the Other is therefore stronger than saying that society influences preferences. The subject is constituted in a symbolic order and receives its own message back in an inverted form. [Sources: Lacan, *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject," pp. 683-690; Seminar VI, early graph sessions.]

This is one reason desire is opaque to consciousness. The subject can often identify what is being demanded but not "whence" it desires. Lacan makes this distinction explicitly in "Subversion of the Subject": the nescience at stake is less ignorance of what one demands than ignorance of the place from which one desires. [Source: Lacan, *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject," p. 689.]

### 3.6 Seminar XI: desire in the gaps of the Other's discourse

Seminar XI reformulates the same field through the interval between signifiers. Lacan says that the desire of the Other is apprehended in what does not work, in the lacks of the Other's discourse. The child's repeated "why?" is read less as an encyclopedic appetite for reasons than as a testing of the adult: why are you telling me this, what is at stake in your saying? The enigma of the adult's desire reappears within the gaps of speech itself. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XI, ch. 16, around Sheridan p. 214 / project PDF physical p. 228.]

The subject replies to this lack with another lack. Lacan's striking example is the child's offering of its own possible disappearance: "Can he lose me?" The fantasy of one's own loss enters as a response to the unknown object of parental desire. This is not a universal literal fantasy that must be consciously present; it exemplifies the logic of separation in which a lack on the side of the subject is brought to bear on the lack encountered in the Other. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XI, ch. 16, pp. 214-215 in the Sheridan edition.]

### 3.7 Fink's systematisation

Fink's *The Lacanian Subject* organises alienation and separation around this confrontation. In his account, alienation concerns submission to the Other as language, while separation concerns confronting the Other as desire. He then treats traversing the fundamental fantasy as a further operation beyond separation. This is a useful pedagogical map, especially for linking Seminar XI to clinical questions, but it is Fink's systematic presentation and should not be substituted word-for-word for Lacan's own changing formulations. [Interpretation: Bruce Fink, *The Lacanian Subject*, ch. 5, "The Subject and the Other's Desire," print p. 49 ff.]

### 3.8 What the formula does not mean

The formula does **not** entail that desire is mere imitation. It does not say that the subject wants only objects already wanted by a rival, although rivalry can be one imaginary form of desire. It does not say that desire is simply a wish to please. It does not imply that the Other knows what it wants. And it does not license the analyst to claim privileged knowledge of another person's hidden motives. Its strongest use is structural: desire is constituted in relation to the Other, and the subject's encounter with the Other's desire opens an enigma that fantasy, symptoms, demands, identifications, and objects attempt to organise. [Sources: Lacan, Seminar VI; *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject," pp. 689-694; Seminar XI, chs. 16-19.]


## 4. `Che vuoi?` / `Que veux-tu?` / "What do you want from me?"

### 4.1 The question enters the seminar

Lacan introduces `Che vuoi?` in Seminar VI through Jacques Cazotte's *Le Diable amoureux*. In the scene as Lacan recalls it, the terrifying apparition answers the evocation with the question "Che vuoi?" — "What do you want?" Lacan uses the scene to mark the point at which the subject does not merely encounter a set of available signifiers but an Other whose desire is at issue. The Other can answer, withhold, and determine the presence of signifiers in the exchange; the question therefore opens the dimension in which the subject must situate desire. [Source: Lacan, Seminar VI, 12 November 1958, project source marker p. 10.]

One week later Lacan returns to the formula and explicitly associates it with the second level of the graph. The question is not simply appended to an already transparent act of speech. It asks whether the subject knows what it is doing when speaking. Lacan's answer, through Freud, is no: the act of speech exceeds the conscious intention that accompanies it. `Che vuoi?` therefore marks a disturbance of the assumption that the speaker controls the meaning and desire borne by the utterance. [Source: Lacan, Seminar VI, 19 November 1958, project source marker p. 22.]

### 4.2 Who is asking whom?

The ordinary translation "What do you want?" is grammatically simple but structurally ambiguous. It may sound as though the subject asks another person to state a preference. Lacan's construction reverses and complicates that everyday picture. The question is the **Other's question** returning to the subject from the place from which an answer or guarantee was expected. It therefore becomes, for the subject, something like "What does the Other want from me?" [Source: Lacan, *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject," p. 690.]

This reversal is essential. The subject approaches the Other for an answer, yet encounters a question. The Other does not provide a final label telling the subject what it is, what it must want, or how its demand will be fully guaranteed. Instead, the subject confronts the opacity of the Other's desire. `Che vuoi?` is therefore not a puzzle whose solution is the discovery of the Other's private intention. It is the formalisation of the fact that the place expected to guarantee meaning returns the problem of desire to the subject. [Sources: Lacan, *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject," pp. 689-691; Seminar VI, 12 and 19 November 1958.]

### 4.3 The graphic question mark

The visual form of Graph 3 makes this logic unusually explicit. The lower level of Graph 2 remains: `s(A)` and `A` organise signification and the locus of the Other, with the imaginary circuit below. Above it, a new curved route rises from `A` along the side marked `d` and returns toward the fantasy formula `($ ◊ a)`. `Che vuoi?` is printed over this upper curve. Lacan says that the added level enters the graph like the outline of a question mark planted in the circle of the capital `A`. [Source: Lacan, *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject," Graph 3, p. 690; visual verification of project PDF physical p. 707.]

[FIGURE: Graph 3 — verified source version]

Source: Jacques Lacan, *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject and the Dialectic of Desire in the Freudian Unconscious".  
Location: English p. 690; project PDF physical p. 707.  
Visible labels: `Chè vuoi?`, `($ ◊ a)`, `d`, `s(A)`, `A`, `m`, `i(a)`, `I(A)`, `$`.  
Directional structure: Graph 2 remains below. From `A`, the added upper pathways curve upward and back toward `($ ◊ a)`, producing the question-mark profile; `d` is marked on the right side of the upper return.  
Conceptual role: the Other's question opens the upper level in which desire and fantasy are articulated.  
Uncertainty: none material. The accented printed form in this edition is `Chè vuoi?`; this file otherwise follows the conventional project title `Che vuoi?`.

The location of `($ ◊ a)` at the return side of the question is decisive. Fantasy is not an unrelated topic introduced after the question has been solved. It is positioned as part of the structural response to the enigma opened by the Other's desire. The subject is barred or fading under the signifier, and fantasy links that divided subject to an object term. The graph thus already indicates why the question "What does the Other want from me?" cannot be answered by a simple proposition. [Source: Lacan, *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject," pp. 690-692.]

### 4.4 From `Che vuoi?` to `Que me veut-Il?`

Seminar X reopens the formula rather than treating it as settled. Lacan recalls `Che vuoi?`, `Que veux-tu?`, and then pushes the question to `Que me veut-Il?`. The French construction allows him to hear both "What does the Other want with me?" and a question about what the Other wants concerning the place of the ego or the subject. This intensifies the relation to anxiety: the subject is not simply uncertain about an external demand but implicated as something potentially wanted by the Other. [Source: Lacan, Seminar X, opening lessons, project source around the early `Che vuoi?` discussion.]

The praying-mantis image that accompanies Seminar X's opening orientation gives this a visceral form. The subject imagines itself before a radically enigmatic Other and does not know what object it may be for that Other. The image should not be literalised into a theory that other people are predators. Its function is formal: anxiety appears where the subject's position in the Other's desire cannot be secured by the usual imaginary and symbolic coordinates. [Source: Lacan, Seminar X, opening lessons on anxiety and the desire of the Other.]

### 4.5 `Che vuoi?` and fantasy

Fantasy supplies a structured relation where the Other's question would otherwise remain radically open. In the graph, `($ ◊ a)` is not the statement "the Other wants object *a*." It is the formula of a relation between the divided subject and an object that functions within desire. Fantasy gives the subject a way of staging its place relative to desire, thereby supporting desire without eliminating the lack that makes desire possible. [Source: Lacan, *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject," pp. 690-692; Seminar VI, fantasy sequence.]

This is why a fantasy can be persistent even when its overt scenario is unpleasant. Its structural function is not reducible to conscious gratification or to imagining something pleasant. Seminar V's reading of Freud's "A Child Is Being Beaten" emphasises the fantasy's transformation, its relation to the signifier, and the way a reduced object/signifying element can persist as a pivot. Seminar VI then makes the fantasy formula central to the problem of desire. [Sources: Lacan, Seminar V, ch. XIII, "Fantasy, Beyond the Pleasure Principle," pp. 216-234; Seminar VI, later fantasy analyses.]

### 4.6 `Che vuoi?` is not mind-reading

The question must not be operationalised as a technique for guessing what a partner, parent, manager, analyst, or institution "really wants." That would restore exactly the kind of hidden complete intention that Lacan's theory of the lacking Other contests. The question is useful because it reveals the structural pressure to supply such an answer and the ways subjects organise themselves around that pressure. [Sources: Lacan, *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject," pp. 690, 693-694; Seminar XI, desire of the Other in the gaps of discourse.]

Nor does `Che vuoi?` licence an analyst to answer the question on the Other's behalf. In "Subversion of the Subject," Lacan explicitly refuses the analyst the position of guaranteeing an ultimate doctrinal truth. The analytic problem is how the subject takes up the question of desire, not how an expert supplies the final content of another's desire. [Source: Lacan, *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject," pp. 693-694.]

The compact relation is therefore: **demand addresses the Other; the Other is encountered as desiring; the Other's desire returns as a question; fantasy organises a response without closing the question once and for all.** Seminar X then shows why anxiety becomes especially important when the screens that normally organise this relation fail or when object *a* comes too close. [Sources: Lacan, Seminar VI; *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject," Graph 3; Seminar X.]


## 5. The Genesis of the Graph of Desire

### 5.1 The graph is constructed, not revealed all at once

The graph of desire should be read as a construction. Lacan says in "Subversion of the Subject" that the graph was made for his seminar on formations of the unconscious and was worked out especially through the structure of jokes in the final term of 1957. Seminar VI likewise reminds the audience that the graph had been put together progressively the previous year and that its uses would become clearer through the study of desire. [Sources: Lacan, *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject," introductory note before the graph sequence; Seminar VI, early sessions on the graph.]

Seminar V's table of contents and later appendix make the progression visible. The seminar moves from wit and the signifier through the paternal metaphor and Oedipus, then to fantasy, desire and jouissance, the formulas of desire, the distinction between desire and demand, the Other's desire, obsessional desire, and the circuits of desire. Appendix A finally reproduces the complete graph and tells the reader that the definitive version is found in the *Écrits* on page 692, preceded by developmental versions on pages 681, 684, and 690. [Source: Lacan, Seminar V, structure of the English edition and Appendix A, English p. 485 ff.; visual inspection of project PDF physical p. 487.]

### 5.2 Why the formations of the unconscious matter

The graph grows from the claim that unconscious formations have a linguistic structure. Jokes, slips, dreams, and symptoms are not treated as containers in which a hidden message sits unchanged. Their effects depend on signifying substitutions, combinations, timing, and retroaction. Lacan's use of Freud's condensation and displacement as points of comparison for metaphor and metonymy therefore provides the technical background for the graph. [Sources: Lacan, *Écrits*, "The Instance of the Letter in the Unconscious"; Seminar V, opening part on wit; background: Freud, *Dream Psychology*, dream-work sections.]

The elementary problem is signification itself. A sentence does not acquire its final sense term by term in a simple forward accumulation. Later elements retroactively determine how earlier elements are understood. Lacan's `point de capiton`, the quilting or button-tie point, names this punctuation effect. Graph 1 models the intersection of the signifying chain with the vector of the subject in a way that makes retroaction central. [Source: Lacan, *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject," pp. 681-682.]

### 5.3 From signification to the Other

Graph 2 names the two crucial intersections `A` and `s(A)`. `A` is the locus of the signifying battery, the Other; `s(A)` is the point at which signification is punctuated as a finished effect. The asymmetry is important: one is a locus and the other a moment or scansion. The graph therefore does not depict two psychological entities communicating. It formalises a route through the field of the signifier. [Source: Lacan, *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject," pp. 682-684, Graph 2.]

At the same time Graph 2 adds the imaginary circuit `i(a) → m` and the relation to `I(A)`, the ego-ideal. The ego's image is not discarded, but it is subordinated to the symbolic articulation. The subject's relation to its specular and egoic forms is therefore represented as a short circuit within a more encompassing signifying structure. [Source: Lacan, *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject," pp. 684-686.]

### 5.4 The graph is not a flowchart of childhood

Because Lacan uses infantile examples and because the diagrams are staged as Graphs 1, 2, 3, and Complete Graph, it is tempting to treat them as chronological developmental stages. That is misleading. The stages are stages in the **construction of the schema** and in the exposition of structural functions. They can illuminate developmental material, but the graph is not a milestone chart in which a person moves once from Graph 1 to Graph 4 and never returns. [Sources: Lacan, Seminar V; Seminar VI; *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject." Interpretation: Eidelsztein, *The Graph of Desire*, chs. 4, 6, and 7.]

The same caution applies to the upper and lower levels. "Lower" does not simply mean biologically earlier and "upper" psychologically later. The complete graph differentiates levels of enunciation, demand/drive, desire, fantasy, lack in the Other, jouissance, and castration. Their vertical placement is a formal distinction within a topology of speech, not a ranking from primitive to advanced personality. [Source: Lacan, *Écrits*, Complete Graph, p. 692.]

### 5.5 Seminar VI: the graph becomes an instrument for desire

At the beginning of Seminar VI, Lacan tells his audience that the graph is a tool for orientation and that its purpose is to distinguish things that are easily confused, including the repressed, desire, and the unconscious. The explicit theme of the year changes the graph's use: the problem is no longer merely how signification and formations of the unconscious work, but how the subject's desire can be located within that structure. [Source: Lacan, Seminar VI, early sessions following 19 November 1958.]

This is where `Che vuoi?` takes on its full importance. The second level is organised around the question arising from the Other's desire. The subject cannot remain at the level of an apparently innocent capture in language, because speech raises the issue of what is wanted beyond what is explicitly said. The graph becomes a way to keep demand, signification, desire, fantasy, and the unconscious in differentiated positions. [Source: Lacan, Seminar VI, 12 and 19 November 1958.]

### 5.6 Eidelsztein's technical reconstruction

Eidelsztein's book is useful because it refuses to treat the graph as an illustration that can be paraphrased away. He organises his exposition around graph theory, topology, the structure of language, Graph 1, Graph 2, Graph 3, fantasy, drive, and `S(Ⱥ)`. His method foregrounds vertices, edges, continuity, and the relation between the graph's form and Lacan's claims. [Interpretation: Alfredo Eidelsztein, *The Graph of Desire*, chs. 1-13.]

That technical insistence is productive but must remain attributed. Eidelsztein sometimes makes stronger structural claims than Lacan explicitly makes at the same point, for example when treating the graph's structure itself in relation to object *a*. Such readings can disclose consequences of the formalism, but they should not be retroactively presented as direct quotations from the Seminar V or *Écrits* passages. [Interpretation: Eidelsztein, *The Graph of Desire*, early chapters on graph theory and later chapters on object *a* and fantasy.]

The safest procedure is therefore double: first reconstruct what each graph visibly adds and what Lacan says it does; then use interpreters to clarify the consequences and disputes. That is the procedure followed in the next section.


## 6. The Graph of Desire, Stage by Stage

### 6.1 Reading conventions

The graph is reproduced here in portable descriptive form rather than as a substitute drawing. The diagrams in the source have been visually checked. Arrow direction, relative placement, and labels matter. The symbol `$` below denotes Lacan's barred subject, not a currency sign. The losange `◊` is retained in formulas such as `($ ◊ a)` and `($ ◊ D)` because it is not adequately replaced by an ordinary English connective. [Source: Lacan, *Écrits*, graph sequence pp. 681, 684, 690, 692; visual verification of project PDF physical pp. 698, 701, 707, 709.]

### 6.2 Graph 1: the elementary cell

[FIGURE: Graph 1 — verified source version]

Source: Jacques Lacan, *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject and the Dialectic of Desire in the Freudian Unconscious".  
Location: English p. 681; project PDF physical p. 698.  
Visible labels: `S`, `S′`, `$`, `Δ`.  
Visible structure: a horizontal-curved signifying vector runs from `S` toward `S′`; a second loop begins from `Δ`, rises through the signifying trajectory, crosses it twice, and descends toward `$`.  
Portable reconstruction:

```text
S  ------------------------------>  S′
          \                    /
           \      loop        /
            \                /
             v              /
             $             Δ

Conceptual relation: the Δ → $ route intersects the S → S′ signifying chain twice.
```

The ASCII spacing is schematic; the verified source image controls the geometry and arrow direction.

Graph 1 is the elementary cell of retroactive signification. Its crucial feature is not the shape of a fish or loop in itself but the double intersection. The signifying chain does not merely transmit a ready-made meaning from left to right. Meaning is punctuated retroactively: the completion of an utterance changes how its earlier elements count. Lacan names the relevant stopping or stitching function the `point de capiton`. [Source: Lacan, *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject," pp. 681-682.]

The second vector concerns the subject in relation to this chain. `Δ` should not be read as a fully formed subject with a private intention entering language from outside and emerging unchanged as `$`. The barred subject at the other end indicates that subjection to the signifier divides the subject. The graph therefore begins by refusing a communication model in which an intact speaker simply encodes a pre-existing thought. [Source: Lacan, *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject," pp. 681-683.]

Seminar VI's early teaching use of the graph emphasises this same point: the graph can distinguish unconscious formations and desire only if the subject is not identified with conscious intention. The question `Che vuoi?` will later arise because the act of speaking exceeds what the speaker knowingly means. [Source: Lacan, Seminar VI, 19 November 1958.]

### 6.3 Graph 2: the Other, punctuation, and the imaginary circuit

[FIGURE: Graph 2 — verified source version]

Source: Jacques Lacan, *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject".  
Location: English p. 684; project PDF physical p. 701.  
Visible labels: `Signifier`, `s(A)`, `A`, `Voice`, `m`, `i(a)`, `I(A)`, `$`.  
Visible pathways:
- lower signifying path: `Signifier → s(A) → A → Voice`;
- an upper return arc from `A` back toward `s(A)`;
- a lower imaginary loop connecting the `$ / I(A)` level to the `A / s(A)` level, marked `i(a)` on the right and `m` on the left;
- `I(A)` and `$` appear as lower endpoints.

Portable reconstruction:

```text
                   A  ---------> Voice
                 /   \
          s(A) <------ A
           ^            \
Signifier ->|             \
            |              i(a)
            m               \
            |------<---------|
            |                |
           I(A)              $
```

This is schematic only; the source figure is authoritative.

`A` is the locus of the Other, the treasure trove of signifiers; `s(A)` is the punctuation at which signification is produced. Lacan insists on their asymmetry: the former is a locus, the latter a moment. The movement from `A` back to `s(A)` represents the retroactive effect by which the message is determined only after traversing the Other. [Source: Lacan, *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject," pp. 682-684.]

Graph 2 also places the imaginary relation inside the symbolic architecture. `i(a)` names the specular image and `m` the ego; `I(A)` is the ego-ideal. The imaginary relation can form a short circuit, which is why apparent self-knowledge at the ego level cannot be equated with the subject of the unconscious. The graph does not abolish the imaginary; it situates its relative function. [Source: Lacan, *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject," pp. 684-686.]

This second graph therefore adds a decisive answer to the question "Where does signification come from?" It comes neither from a private inner intention nor from a one-to-one code between signs and things. It is produced through a signifying battery located in the Other and through punctuation/retroaction. Desire has not yet received its full upper-level articulation, but its conditions are being constructed. [Source: Lacan, *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject," pp. 682-686.]

### 6.4 Graph 3: the question of the Other's desire

[FIGURE: Graph 3 — verified source version]

Source: Jacques Lacan, *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject".  
Location: English p. 690; project PDF physical p. 707.  
Visible labels: `Chè vuoi?`, `($ ◊ a)`, `d`, `s(A)`, `A`, `m`, `i(a)`, `I(A)`, `$`.  
Visible pathways: Graph 2 remains below. From `A`, two large return curves rise into an upper register and curve toward `($ ◊ a)`, with `d` printed on the right-hand ascent. The outer curve gives the added level the form of a question mark.  
Portable search reconstruction:

```text
                     Chè vuoi?
                       _______
                     /         \
             ($ ◊ a) <--------- d
                |                 \
              s(A) <------------  A ----> [voice]
                |       ...       |
               m                 i(a)
                |                 |
               I(A)               $
```

Again, this is not a geometric replacement for the source.

Graph 3 marks the point at which the Other ceases to function merely as the locus through which a message is punctuated and appears as an Other whose desire poses a question. The curve is not decorative. Lacan explicitly describes the superimposed level as the outline of a question mark planted in the circle of `A`. [Source: Lacan, *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject," p. 690.]

The left-hand term `($ ◊ a)` means that fantasy is already implicated in the question. The subject is not given a transparent answer to "What am I for the Other?" Instead, a relation between the barred subject and an object term structures how desire can be sustained. `d` marks desire on the right-hand route. Desire and fantasy therefore occupy positions generated by the encounter with the Other rather than forming a private interior layer independent of speech. [Source: Lacan, *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject," pp. 690-692.]

Seminar X later revisits precisely this hinge and reformulates `Che vuoi?` as `Que me veut-Il?`. That later return confirms that Graph 3 should not be treated as a one-time historical curiosity; it formalises a problem Lacan continues to transform. [Source: Lacan, Seminar X, opening lessons.]

### 6.5 The complete graph: enunciation, drive, lack, jouissance, castration

[FIGURE: Complete Graph — verified source version]

Source: Jacques Lacan, *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject".  
Location: English p. 692; project PDF physical p. 709.  
Visible labels:
- upper level: `Jouissance`, `S(Ⱥ)`, `($ ◊ D)`, `Castration`;
- inter-level terms: `($ ◊ a)` on the left and `d` on the right;
- lower level: `Signifier`, `s(A)`, `A`, `Voice`, `m`, `i(a)`, `I(A)`, `$`.

Visible directional structure:
- two opposed curved paths connect `S(Ⱥ)` and `($ ◊ D)` across the upper level;
- `S(Ⱥ)` descends toward `s(A)` on the left;
- `($ ◊ D)` connects toward `A` on the right;
- `($ ◊ a)` and `d` occupy the inter-level routes;
- Graph 2 remains as the lower architecture.

Portable reconstruction:

```text
          Jouissance                          Castration
              \                                  /
             S(Ⱥ)   <==================>   ($ ◊ D)
               | \                            / |
         ($ ◊ a)|  \________________________/  | d
               |                                |
              s(A)  <======================>    A ----> Voice
               | \                            / |
               m  \__________<_____________/ i(a)
               |                                |
              I(A)                              $

         Signifier enters the lower signifying chain at s(A).
```

The double arrows in the ASCII representation indicate opposed source pathways, not an assertion that every relation is reciprocal.

The upper-right formula `($ ◊ D)` is the subject in relation to demand as it appears in the drive. Lacan says that the drive is what becomes of demand when the subject vanishes from it; demand disappears as an ordinary request, while the cut remains. This is why the upper chain should not be read as a second conscious message hidden above the first. It concerns unconscious enunciation and the grammatical organisation of drive. [Source: Lacan, *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject," pp. 692-693.]

The upper-left `S(Ⱥ)` is the signifier of a lack in the Other. The barred `A` is not an empirical claim that a particular authority is flawed; it formalises that the Other as treasury of signifiers contains no final signifier that guarantees the system from outside. Lacan connects this to the proposition that there is no Other of the Other. [Source: Lacan, *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject," pp. 693-694.]

The location of `Jouissance` and `Castration` prevents the graph from being reduced to communication theory. The complete graph puts the production of meaning into relation with the limits and losses bound up with speaking, drive, desire, and jouissance. A merely linguistic paraphrase that omits jouissance and castration therefore misses what the final graph adds. [Source: Lacan, *Écrits*, Complete Graph and surrounding discussion, pp. 692-697.]

### 6.6 The Seminar V formula rows

Seminar V anticipates parts of this architecture through formula rows written on the board at the beginning of "The Formulas of Desire." Visual inspection of the project PDF confirms three horizontal formulae linking `d`, `$ ◊ a`, `i(a)`, and `m`; `D`, `A ◊ d`, `s(A)`, and `I`; and `Δ`, `$ ◊ D`, `S(Ⱥ)`, and `Φ`. The printed arrows are bidirectional in the central portions. Because the typography is more legible visually than in parsed text, the formulas should not be reconstructed from OCR alone. [Source: Lacan, Seminar V, ch. XVII, project PDF physical p. 290 / printed page shown as 303 in that scan; visual verification.]

[FIGURE: Seminar V — three formulas of desire, visually verified]

```text
d  →  $ ◊ a   ⇄   i(a)  ←  m
D  →  A ◊ d   ⇄   s(A)  ←  I
Δ  →  $ ◊ D   ⇄   S(Ⱥ)  ←  Φ
```

This transcription is a portability aid. It records the visible terms and arrow pattern of the inspected page; it should not be treated as a replacement for Lacan's subsequent explanations in that chapter.

### 6.7 What the graph does and does not do

The graph is a formal map of relations among signification, subject, Other, imaginary identifications, desire, fantasy, demand/drive, lack in the Other, jouissance, and castration. It is not an anatomical map, a developmental ladder, a personality test, or a causal flowchart in which one starts at the bottom and ends at the top. Its arrows are not ordinary chronological arrows. Retroaction is constitutive from Graph 1 onward. [Sources: Lacan, *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject," pp. 681-697; Interpretation: Eidelsztein, *The Graph of Desire*, chs. 4-7.]

Eidelsztein's technical contribution is strongest when it forces attention back onto those relations rather than permitting the graph to dissolve into prose. His chapter sequence treats Graph 1, Graph 2, and Graph 3 separately, and he uses graph theory and topology to ask what formal invariants are carried across the transformations. The gain is precision about structure; the risk is that a formal consequence may be heard as though Lacan had explicitly stated it at the same historical moment. This synthesis therefore uses Eidelsztein as a technical interpreter, not as a proxy author for Lacan. [Interpretation: Eidelsztein, *The Graph of Desire*, chs. 4, 6, 7; see also chs. 8-13.]


## 7. Fantasy and the Fantasy Formula

### 7.1 Fantasy is a structure, not simply a daydream

Lacan's use of *fantasy* (*fantasme*) does not designate merely a conscious imaginative scene. A conscious fantasy can of course provide material for analysis, but the technical concept concerns a structure by which the subject's relation to desire is organised. This is why the graph gives fantasy a formula, `($ ◊ a)`, and places it at a strategic point in the relation between the Other's question and desire. [Sources: Lacan, *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject," pp. 690-692; Seminar VI, 15 April 1959 and related sessions.]

The two terms should be read first in their minimal formal sense. `$` denotes the divided or barred subject, the subject marked by the signifier. `a` is the object term whose theoretical status changes across Lacan and becomes the mature object *a*. The `◊` or losange cannot be safely translated by one English word. Seminar X explicitly exploits its composite form to evoke conjunction and disjunction, and relations of greater-than and less-than. It functions as a relation operator whose polyvalence should not be collapsed into "has," "wants," or "looks at." [Source: Lacan, Seminar X, source pp. 174-175, discussion of the fantasy formula and the rhomb.]

### 7.2 Seminar V: fantasy, signifier, and Freud's beating fantasy

Seminar V's chapter "Fantasy, Beyond the Pleasure Principle" returns to Freud's "A Child Is Being Beaten." Lacan stresses the transformations of the scenario and the way the final fantasy can become reduced and stabilised. His discussion isolates a material signifying element, the whip, as a persistent pivot. The point is not that the fantasy is decoded by matching each image with a fixed symbol. Its form records a relation of the subject to the signifier and to desire that survives transformations of the cast of characters and conscious meanings. [Source: Lacan, Seminar V, ch. XIII, "Fantasy, Beyond the Pleasure Principle," English pp. 216-234.]

This makes the fantasy's apparent perversity a poor guide to structural diagnosis. A beating scene may occur in neurosis without turning the person into a perverse structure, just as an aggressive image does not directly reveal an instinctual essence. Lacan explicitly uses Freud's analysis to show that repression, compromise, and return of the repressed are involved in such fantasies. [Source: Lacan, Seminar V, ch. XIII, especially the opening and middle sections.]

The same chapter connects the fantasy to entry into the world of desire. The final fantasy's generalised form makes visible an essential relation between subject and signifier. This prepares the later formula but should not be rewritten retrospectively as though Seminar V already contained every later determination of object *a*. [Source: Lacan, Seminar V, ch. XIII, later sections; chronological qualification from Seminar X.]

### 7.3 Seminar VI: fantasy as the support and regulation of desire

Seminar VI places fantasy directly at the "abutment" of the subject's question. In the Hamlet sequence, Lacan describes Hamlet's drama as a drama of desire in relation to the desire of the Other. He then says that the imaginary regulation supporting desire is symbolised by the barred subject in the presence of the object, the configuration he calls fantasy. Fantasy appears as a final or limiting term of desire while remaining dependent on an unconscious signifying circuit. [Source: Lacan, Seminar VI, 15 April 1959, source pp. 213-215 in the project transcription.]

This is why fantasy can look paradoxical or absurd when taken as a conscious narrative. Its structural intelligibility lies elsewhere. The manifest scene may be ridiculous, painful, repetitive, erotic, humiliating, heroic, or mundane, yet still organise a stable position for the subject relative to an object and to the Other's desire. The question is not "What fantasy story do I like?" but "What relation is being staged between the divided subject, the object, and the desire that cannot be fully stated?" [Sources: Lacan, Seminar VI, fantasy/Hamlet sequence; *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject," pp. 690-692.]

Seminar VI also provides an important caution against personifying literary examples. Lacan says explicitly that Hamlet is not an obsessional simply because the play can illuminate obsessional structure: Hamlet is a poetic creation that demonstrates aspects of neurosis. The same restraint is necessary whenever fantasy formulas are used outside the clinic. A formal analogy is not a licence for casual diagnosis. [Source: Lacan, Seminar VI, 8 April 1959, project source around p. 204.]

### 7.4 Fantasy in Graph 3

Graph 3 places `($ ◊ a)` at the left end of the upper question-mark structure associated with `Che vuoi?`. This location suggests a structural answer without implying a verbal answer. The subject does not settle the Other's desire by learning a sentence about it; fantasy supplies a relation in which the subject can occupy a place relative to an object. [Source: Lacan, *Écrits*, Graph 3, p. 690; surrounding text pp. 690-691.]

Lacan says that the formula links the subject's fading under the signifier to the object and warns that the algorithm can support several readings as long as the algebraic constraints are respected. This warning is important. A formula such as `($ ◊ a)` is not a pictogram with one translation. It is valuable because it preserves a relation that ordinary prose tends to oversimplify. [Source: Lacan, *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject," p. 691.]

The relation of desire to fantasy is consequently not the relation of an appetite to its imaginary picture. Fantasy supports desire by giving form to the subject's position in relation to the object and the Other's desire. The object is not simply what the subject hopes eventually to obtain. In Seminar X, this distinction becomes explicit when *a* is reformulated as the cause of desire. [Sources: Lacan, *Écrits*, pp. 690-692; Seminar X, ch. VIII.]

### 7.5 Seminar X: fantasy, remainder, and anxiety

Seminar X provides a more formal account of the two terms. Lacan describes the barred subject as one term of fantasy and *a* as an irreducible remainder that resists assimilation to the signifier. The fantasy supports desire because the subject's relation to this remainder gives a structure to desire. The object is not a second subject or a fully signified commodity; it marks what is left over in signifierisation. [Source: Lacan, Seminar X, source pp. 174-176.]

Anxiety occupies a median position between jouissance and desire in this account. Lacan says that the constitution of desire involves an anxiety phase even when it cannot be phenomenologically isolated. His return to Freud's beating fantasy is significant: Freud's missing or reconstructed phase becomes an example of how a fantasy can have a structural moment that is not simply available as a conscious memory. [Source: Lacan, Seminar X, source pp. 174-176.]

This also clarifies why fantasy can ordinarily protect the subject from anxiety. To say it "protects" does not mean it is comforting. A painful fantasy can still provide coordinates: it gives the subject a position and an object relation. Anxiety becomes especially important when the usual structuring relation is disturbed, when the object does not remain in its accustomed masked place, or when the subject's status for the Other becomes too immediate. [Sources: Lacan, Seminar X, opening lessons and later discussion of fantasy and object *a*.]

### 7.6 Fantasy is not the same as symptom

Fantasy and symptom intersect but should not be equated. Symptoms are formations that can carry signifying determinations, repetitions, and forms of satisfaction; fantasy is the more fundamental relation through which desire is supported. Seminar VI's Hamlet analysis and Seminar V's clinical work make clear that a symptom can be read through the subject's relation to desire without being identical with the fantasy formula. [Sources: Lacan, Seminar V, clinical section; Seminar VI, Hamlet/fantasy sequence.]

Fink's later formulation of "traversing the fundamental fantasy" systematises a clinical endpoint in which the analysand's relation to the Other's desire and object *a* is reconfigured. This is useful as an interpretive development, but it should not be inserted into the 1958 graph as if it were already the explicit aim of the schema at that date. [Interpretation: Fink, *The Lacanian Subject*, chs. 5-7.]

### 7.7 A portable reading of `($ ◊ a)`

A defensible research shorthand is:

```text
$  = the subject insofar as divided / barred by the signifier
◊  = a formal relation that must not be reduced to one ordinary-language verb
a  = the object term; in later Lacan, object a as remainder/cause of desire

($ ◊ a) = the structural relation in fantasy by which the divided subject's desire is supported in relation to object a.
```

This shorthand is intentionally incomplete. The formula's value lies precisely in resisting the temptation to turn fantasy into a sentence such as "I want object X" or "I imagine scene Y." [Sources: Lacan, *Écrits*, p. 691; Seminar X, pp. 174-176.]


## 8. Object *a*: From Object of Desire to Cause of Desire

### 8.1 Why chronology is indispensable

Object *a* is especially vulnerable to retrospective flattening because Lacan reuses the letter *a* while changing its theoretical work. Earlier Lacan uses *a* for the little other and imaginary relations. By the 1960s object *a* becomes a remainder and, in Seminar X, is explicitly named the **cause of desire**. Secondary reference works correctly warn that the concept has several avatars and should be followed historically. [Interpretations: *A Compendium of Lacanian Terms*, "Object a," p. 122 ff.; Fink, *The Lacanian Subject*, discussions of object *a*.]

The fact that later object *a* clarifies earlier problems does not mean the mature concept was fully present from the start. A responsible synthesis therefore distinguishes at least three moments: the earlier imaginary *a*; the object term in the fantasy formula and the lost/metonymic object of desire in the late 1950s; and Seminar X's explicit causal formulation. [Sources: Lacan, Seminar V; Seminar VI; *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject"; Seminar X.]

### 8.2 The late-1950s problem: the object does not satisfy desire once and for all

Seminar V repeatedly separates the object of desire from a natural object that would complete the subject. An early formulation says that desire is always desire for another thing, for what is lacking, and connects the object to the Freudian motif of a lost object that must be refound. Even before the mature theory of cause, desire is therefore organised around displacement and lack rather than final adequation between appetite and object. [Source: Lacan, Seminar V, early discussion of metaphor/metonymy and the object of desire; later chapters on desire.]

The fantasy formula deepens this problem. The object term in `($ ◊ a)` is not simply an item at the end of a vector of intentionality. It belongs to a relation in which the subject itself is barred and in which desire is supported. Seminar VI's use of fantasy in the Hamlet sequence makes clear that the subject's ability to desire depends on how the object functions within this relation. [Sources: Lacan, Seminar VI, 15 April 1959; *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject," pp. 690-692.]

### 8.3 Seminar X: the causal reversal

Seminar X states the distinction with exceptional clarity. Lacan asks whether the object of desire should be conceived in the ordinary intentional model as something placed in front of a thought or aiming. He rejects that model for object *a*. The object *a* is to be conceived as the cause of desire, and he says that the object lies **behind** desire. [Source: Lacan, Seminar X, ch. VIII, "The Cause of Desire," source pp. 101-102.]

This is a conceptual reversal. If one says "I desire the red car," the red car is the apparent intentional object. Object *a* is not therefore a tiny hidden red car behind the conscious one. The causal object names the structural remainder or condition that sets desire in motion and allows it to fasten onto empirical objects. Desire can move from object to object precisely because no empirical target is identical with its cause. [Source: Lacan, Seminar X, ch. VIII, source pp. 101-104; synthesis of the causal distinction.]

Lacan's fetish example is designed to make the difference tangible. He says the shoe or breast as fetish is not simply what is desired; the fetish functions as a condition that **causes** desire, while desire can attach elsewhere. The example is not a universal model of everyone's sexuality, but it makes the causal placement visible. [Source: Lacan, Seminar X, ch. VIII, source p. 103.]

### 8.4 Object *a* as remainder

Seminar X also links object *a* to what falls away or remains irreducible in signifierisation. In the schema around source pp. 174-176, *a* is the remainder that cannot be assimilated to a signifier. This remainder comes to found the desiring subject in fantasy. The causal object is therefore not a secret signified. It is defined in part by resistance to complete symbolisation. [Source: Lacan, Seminar X, source pp. 174-176.]

This helps explain the otherwise paradoxical status of *a*: it is indispensable to the structure yet cannot simply be named as one stable empirical thing. Lacan's lists of partial objects and later additions such as gaze and voice do not mean that object *a* is a category of small body parts. These objects can occupy the *a* function because of the way loss, cut, and drive are structured. [Sources: Lacan, *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject," pp. 692-693; Seminar X, later lessons on the forms of object *a*.]

### 8.5 Seminar XI: lost part, partial drive, and "in you more than you"

Seminar XI rearticulates loss through the two lacks involved in sexuality and subjectivation. Lacan rejects the myth that love simply seeks a complementary other half and instead describes the search for a part lost by the subject's advent as a sexed living being. The partial drive circles objects without those objects becoming a final complement. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XI, ch. 16, especially pp. 204-205 in the Sheridan edition.]

The concluding title "In you more than you" captures another side of object *a*: the object is not valued because it exhausts the beloved person's properties but because something in the other exceeds the inventory of qualities. The phrase should not be converted into romantic mysticism; in the seminar it belongs to the relation among transference, object *a*, and the analyst's desire. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XI, ch. 20, "In you more than you," pp. 263 ff.]

### 8.6 Object *a* and the desire of the Other

A strong later Lacanian reading, especially in Fink, associates object *a* with the Other's desire insofar as that enigmatic desire functions as the subject's cause. This is useful if kept precise: it does not mean that object *a* is whatever another person consciously wants. The cause is linked to the subject's encounter with the Other's lack/desire and to the remainder produced in the subject's own constitution. [Interpretation: Fink, *The Lacanian Subject*, chs. 5-7 and glossary; primary controls: Seminar X and Seminar XI.]

Eidelsztein makes a different technical emphasis. He argues that the graph's formal structure can be read in relation to object *a* even though the mature phrase "object *a* as cause of desire" is not explicitly inscribed in the graph itself at the moment of its original construction. This reading is valuable precisely as a retrospective structural interpretation; it should not erase the chronological fact that Seminar X makes the causal formula explicit later. [Interpretation: Eidelsztein, *The Graph of Desire*, early chapters on object *a* and graph theory; chronological control: Lacan, Seminar X.]

### 8.7 What object *a* is not

Object *a* is not simply:

- the person one desires;
- the commodity one wants;
- a missing body part in a literal sense;
- an ideal self-image;
- an unconscious object whose identity could be discovered once and for all;
- a synonym for "lack" without remainder;
- the same as the phallus in every context.

It is a formal object with changing articulations across Lacan. In the mature causal formulation, it names the remainder/condition that causes desire and supports fantasy, not the ordinary target that promises to satisfy desire. [Sources: Lacan, Seminar X, ch. VIII and source pp. 174-176; *A Compendium of Lacanian Terms*, "Object a".]


## 9. Lack in the Other

### 9.1 `S(Ⱥ)` and the missing guarantee

The complete graph places `S(Ⱥ)` at the upper left. The barred `A` denotes a lack in the Other, and `S(Ⱥ)` is the signifier associated with that lack. Lacan's surrounding argument makes the stake explicit: the Other as treasure of signifiers does not contain a final signifier that could guarantee the value of all the others from outside the system. There is no "Other of the Other" that would close the chain. [Source: Lacan, *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject," pp. 692-694.]

This is a logical claim about the signifying structure, not a banal observation that authority figures are imperfect. A parent may be competent or incompetent, a law stable or unstable, an institution reliable or corrupt; none of those empirical properties exhaust `Ⱥ`. Even an apparently flawless authority could not supply a metalanguage that finally guarantees the whole field of signification from outside it. [Source: Lacan, *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject," pp. 693-694.]

### 9.2 Lack in the Other is not absence of the Other

To say that the Other is barred does not mean the Other disappears. Language, law, institutions, kinship, conventions, and the discourse of others remain effective. The point is that their efficacy does not amount to total consistency. This distinction prevents two opposite errors: imagining a complete Other who knows, and imagining that Lacan therefore denies symbolic authority altogether. [Sources: Lacan, *Écrits*, Graph 2 and Complete Graph; Seminar XI, field of the Other.]

The subject depends on the Other while encountering this lack. That dependence is why the lack matters. If the Other were irrelevant, its inconsistency would pose no problem. The subject seeks signifiers, recognition, and orientation in the Other, yet the final signifier that would state once and for all "what I am" and "what the Other wants of me" is unavailable. [Sources: Lacan, *Écrits*, "Subversion of the Subject," pp. 690-694; Seminar XI, chs. 16-17.]

### 9.3 `Che vuoi?` and the barred Other
