| Object-choice | Object-finding/refinding; early care marks later choice | Desire increasingly distinguished from ordinary object relation | Choice mediated by fantasy, jouissance, symptom and contingency | Reconstructs anaclitic/narcissistic choice and love triangles | Connects repeated choice to symptom and singular jouissance | Resemblance to parents is not a sufficient interpretation |
| Demand for love | Not formalised in this vocabulary | Demand exceeds need; every demand also requests love | Remains relevant within jouissance/nonrelation framework | Shows how demands for recognition/knowledge can converge on love | Analytic transference deliberately exposes demand | Concrete needs must not be dismissed as `just demand` |
| Desire of the Other | Anticipated through object-dependence and repetition, but not in Lacan's form | `Che vuoi?`; desire is desire of the Other; fantasy answers opacity | Reworked through object *a*, sexuation and jouissance | Unpacks desire to be desired and separation | Uses it in differential clinic and love | Do not treat partner's desire as completely knowable |
| Metaphor of love | No direct equivalent | Seminar VIII: beloved becomes lover; lack is constitutive | Retained but displaced by later object/jouissance theory | Uses it mainly through Lacan | Returns to it in transference discussion | Not a moral ranking of who loves more |
| *Agalma* | No direct equivalent | Hidden precious partial object in Seminar VIII | Object *a* becomes more formal cause | Clarifies move from imaginary object to real cause | Reuses *agalma* in transference | *Agalma* is not a list of attractive traits |
| Transference-love | Fink reconstructs Freud's view of transferred/repeated love | Seminar VIII makes love the mainspring of transference | Seminar XI ties love to subject supposed to know | Emphasises supposition of knowledge and demand for love | Distinguishes old/repetitive love and `new` transference love | Analytic love is not a template for ordinary reciprocity |
| Jealousy | Triangles/repetition mediated mainly through Fink in this project | Mirror rivalry; Dora's jealous object can conceal rival-identification | Can be reread through object *a* and Other's desire | Rival may be object of fascination/identification | Uses hysteria/femininity distinctions where relevant | Cacciali: third can be socialised or destructive; no automatic diagnosis |
| Sexual nonrelation | No direct formula | Not yet formalised as such | No complementary sexual ratio; love/symptom make ties in its absence | Explains `no relationship` as no writable complementarity | Develops no sexual contract, symptom and couple | Does not mean relationships or intercourse do not exist |
| Love and nonrelation | Love/object-dependence creates happiness and vulnerability | Love structured by lack, demand, metaphor, *agalma* | Love `makes up for` absent sexual relation without writing it | Emphasises dissymmetry of partners | Love may shelter singular Otherness | Do not treat love as a cure for structural nonrelation |
| Partner as symptom | No late-Lacanian equivalent | Symptom mainly signifying formation | Late symptom/jouissance framework permits broader function | Touches symptom/desire but not central to partner thesis | Explicitly says partner can come into symptom-place; develops `a woman, symptom` | Not `partner = pathology`; not automatically symmetrical |
| Partner as sinthome | No equivalent | No equivalent | Seminar XXIII: sinthome as supplementary knot/repair | Not primary File 41 anchor | Relationship extensions mainly pass through symptom/prosthesis | No direct project primary proof for universal `partner-sinthome` |
| End of analysis and love | Repetition/object-choice remain central | Analyst must not gratify/idealise transference | Fall of subject supposed to know; late change in relation to symptom | Less dependence on knowing Other | Repetitive restrictions can loosen; jouissance question remains | No guarantee of harmony, marriage or separation |

## Internal cross-reference index

### Love
See Sections 1, 3-7, 13-14, 17-20. Love is treated across narcissistic identification, demand, metaphor, transference, the nonrelation and symptom. The file deliberately resists a single definition.

### Narcissism / imaginary identification
See Sections 2-3 and 10. Primary anchors are Freud's ego/object-boundary discussion and Lacan's `Mirror Stage`; Fink supplies the explicit same/different love-rivalry gloss.

### Ego ideal / idealisation
See Sections 2, 7 and 18. The project primary Freud gap means detailed 1914 narcissism material is mediated through Fink.

### Demand
See Sections 4, 7-9 and 18. The central primary location is Lacan's `Subversion of the Subject`, pp. 689-691.

### Desire / desire of the Other
See Sections 4, 8-9 and 11. `Che vuoi?` is treated as the subject's problem with the Other's opaque desire, not merely a request for information.

### `Che vuoi?`
See Section 8; also Sections 4 and 9. Primary anchors: Seminar VI opening and *Ecrits*, `Subversion`, pp. 689-691.

### Fantasy
See Sections 8-12 and 18. Fantasy is a support/answer to the desire of the Other and a mediator of sexual relation; the full graph/fantasy formalism is reserved to File 40.

### *Agalma*
See Sections 5-7. It names a precious partial object supposed in the other and prepares object *a*; it is not a whole-person trait list.

### Object *a*
See Sections 6, 9, 12 and 15-16. Object *a* is cause/partial object and must not be equated with the empirical partner or with the symptom.

### Transference / subject supposed to know
See Section 7 and Sections 18-19. Seminar VIII supplies *agalma* and transferential disparity; Seminar XI supplies the knowledge/love articulation.

### Jealousy
See Section 10. Primary Lacan anchors: `Mirror Stage` and `Presentation on Transference`; clinical elaboration: Paule Cacciali.

### Hysteria / obsession
See Section 11. These are structural logics, not labels for difficult partner behaviour. Soler is the main project interpreter for hysteria/femininity.

### Sexual nonrelation
See Sections 12-13 and 20-21. File 42 owns the full formal treatment. Here the focus is the consequence for love and couples.

### Feminine love / marriage
See Section 14. Claims about modern marriage, claimants of love and singular couple-love are explicitly attributed to Soler.

### Symptom / partner as symptom
See Section 15. Strongest project relationship source: Soler, Chs. 15-16. The partner can occupy symptom-function; this does not reduce the person to pathology.

### Sinthome / partner-sinthome
See Section 16. Primary Seminar XXIII supports supplementary knotting/repair; a universal partner-sinthome doctrine was not directly verified in the project primary source.

### Jouissance / ravage
See Section 17. Full sexuation/jouissance theory is reserved to File 42. `Ravage` is not used as a casual synonym for a harmful relationship.

### Analysis and love
See Sections 18-19. Possible changes concern demand, fantasy, repetition, idealisation and symptom; no promise of complementarity or relationship success is made.

## Provenance / source note

This file was constructed exclusively from the project source vault and the corpus production constitution. No open-web research was used. Primary claims about Jacques Lacan are tied to the project *Ecrits* or seminar editions/translations. Claims attributed to Bruce Fink, Colette Soler and Paule Cacciali remain explicitly secondary or clinical. Freud claims are direct only where the project Freud texts support them; detailed `On Narcissism` and transference-love reconstructions are identified as mediated through Fink because those Freud primary works were not separately present in the working vault. [Sources: File 41 provenance ledger and source packets SP01-SP12.]

Seminar XX and Seminar XXIII are scan-heavy. Their relevant pages were handled through targeted rendering and visual inspection rather than global OCR. For Seminar XX, the key visually verified windows were Chapter IV, `Love and the Signifier`, printed pp. 38-50, and Chapter VII, `A Love Letter`, printed pp. 78-89. For Seminar XXIII, targeted opening pages were inspected for *sinthome*, Joyce and supplementary knot/repair. [Processing source: File 41 boundary map.]

## Processing notes and uncertainties

1. **Primary Freud gap:** The project working set did not contain standalone copies of Freud's `On Narcissism` or `Observations on Transference-Love`. Fink's article is therefore the immediate source for detailed exposition of those works. No direct page citations to absent Freud editions were invented.

2. **Seminar VI translation:** The project English Seminar VI file is treated as an alternative/private seminar translation. Where possible, `Che vuoi?` claims are cross-anchored to the authorised Fink *Ecrits* translation of `Subversion of the Subject`.

3. **Seminar XX source quality:** Targeted rendered-page inspection was used. No global OCR was performed. The full sexuation formulas are not reproduced here because File 42 owns their formal reconstruction.

4. **Partner-sinthome:** No direct universal `partner as sinthome` proposition was verified in the inspected project Seminar XXIII pages. The file therefore distinguishes Soler's explicit partner-as-symptom development from any later partner-sinthome extension.

5. **Gender and sexuation:** Historical source terminology is retained where needed but is not converted into contemporary personality or identity stereotypes. Full sexuation interpretation belongs in File 42.

6. **Clinical application:** Structural terms such as hysteria, obsession, paranoia, symptom and ravage are not used as casual diagnoses of real people or couples. They require case-specific analytic evidence.

7. **Normative scope:** The file does not give relationship advice and does not rank marriage, separation, monogamy or other consensual arrangements as analytically superior. The relevant Lacanian question is the subject's relation to desire, fantasy, symptom, jouissance and the Other.


## Conceptual distinctions quick reference

### Person, object and cause
The **partner as person** is a speaking subject with an independent history and desire. The **imaginary other** is the partner insofar as he or she is encountered through likeness, image and rivalry. **Object *a*** is not the partner but a cause/remainder that the partner can support in fantasy. **Agalma** is Seminar VIII's precious partial object supposed in the other and a precursor to the more formal object *a*. Keeping these terms separate prevents whole persons from being reduced to functions in another person's psychic economy. [Primary sources: Lacan, Seminar VIII, Chs. X-XIII; Seminar XI, Ch. XX.]

### Love, desire and demand
**Demand** is an address to the Other that carries more than the concrete object requested and can become demand for love. **Desire** emerges in the gap opened by demand and cannot be satisfied as a finite need. **Love** is neither of these: it can answer lack with a signifying gift/address and can transform beloved into lover, while still recruiting demand and desire. The terms overlap in experience but should never be substituted for one another in analysis. [Primary sources: Lacan, *Ecrits*, `Subversion`; Seminar VIII, Ch. III.]

### Drive and jouissance
**Drive** concerns repetitive circuits of partial satisfaction. **Jouissance** names satisfaction beyond ordinary pleasure and can be present in suffering or repetition. A partner may be the occasion or support of jouissance without being its transparent source. Seminar XX's insistence that the jouissance of the Other's body is not the sign of love directly blocks the equation of sexual satisfaction and love. [Primary sources: Lacan, Seminar XI, Chs. XIII-XV; Seminar XX, Ch. IV.]

### Symptom and sinthome
**Symptom** in Lacan traverses several meanings: signifying formation, compromise, return of the repressed, and singular fixation of jouissance. **Sinthome** in Seminar XXIII intensifies the late dimension of singular tying and supplementary consistency. Soler's explicit relationship thesis concerns the partner entering the place of the symptom; a universal partner-sinthome thesis was not directly verified in the project primary source. [Primary source: Lacan, Seminar XXIII. Interpretive source: Soler, Ch. 16.]

### Reciprocity and symmetry
A couple can be reciprocal at the level of care, consent, promise and exchange while remaining **structurally dissymmetrical** in unconscious desire and jouissance. Lacan's claim that the sexual relation is not written does not abolish reciprocity as an ethical/social practice. It denies that reciprocal practice rests on a naturally complementary sexual formula. [Primary source: Lacan, Seminar XX. Interpretive source: Fink, Ch. 8.]

### Lack and deprivation
**Lack** in Lacan is structural and bound to signification/desire. It should not be equated with material neglect or deprivation. A subject can have structural lack while receiving adequate care, and a genuinely deprived subject needs the deprivation recognised rather than reinterpreted as an abstract lesson about desire. [Primary background: Lacan, *Ecrits*, `Subversion`; Seminar X.]

### Love and ethics
Lacanian theory does not make intensity a measure of ethical value. Intense love can coexist with domination, idealisation, sacrifice or ravage. Conversely, a less dramatic attachment can be sustaining. The analytic account of love describes structure; ethical and legal evaluation of acts retains its own criteria. [Primary source: Lacan, *Ecrits*, `Direction of the Treatment`; interpretive source: Soler, Chs. 12 and 17.]

### Couple and social bond
The couple is one social form among others. Freud's *Civilization and Its Discontents* already distinguishes sexual pair-love from aim-inhibited bonds that support wider society. Soler later asks how singular love can coexist with contractual and group forms. Psychoanalysis therefore does not isolate the couple from law, kinship, work, economics or collective ideals, even though File 41 focuses on its libidinal structure. [Primary source: Freud, *Civilization and Its Discontents*. Interpretive source: Soler, Chs. 12-14.]


## Research use: how to query this file without flattening the corpus

A useful Notebook query should normally specify the level of claim sought. Asking `What does Lacan say about love?` invites the retrieval system to merge several periods. Better questions distinguish the source period: `How does Seminar VIII define the metaphor of love?`, `How does Seminar XI connect transference to the subject supposed to know?`, or `What changes in Seminar XX once the sexual nonrelation is explicit?` The answer can then be compared across periods rather than treated as one doctrine. [Source: chronological structure of Sections 5-7, 12-13 and 20.]

Questions about **partner as symptom** should also demand attribution. A robust query is: `What is directly supported by Lacan, what is Soler's development, and what is an inference when the partner is called a symptom or sinthome?` This forces the research system to preserve the distinction between Seminar XXIII's sinthome topology and Soler's explicit relationship-focused symptom formulations. [Primary source: Lacan, Seminar XXIII. Interpretive source: Soler, Chs. 15-17.]

Queries about **hysteria, obsession or jealousy** should avoid person-level diagnosis. Instead of `Is this person hysterical?`, a source-grounded research question is `What structural function does Soler assign to maintaining unsatisfied desire in her account of hysteria?` or `How does Cacciali distinguish imaginary identification with the rival from simple competition?` Such queries retrieve theory without converting it into a label for someone outside an analytic case. [Interpretive source: Soler, Ch. 4. Clinical source: Cacciali, Ch. 17.]

Queries about **marriage and sexual difference** should likewise identify Soler as interpreter. Her claims about the modern decline of older symbolic guarantees and the increasing burden placed on love are historically situated analyses, not timeless Lacanian propositions. A comparison with Seminar XX should ask which elements are Lacan's formal nonrelation and which are Soler's social interpretation. [Primary source: Lacan, Seminar XX. Interpretive source: Soler, Chs. 12-14.]

Queries about **analysis and relationship outcomes** should ask for structural effects rather than advice. `What repetitive conditions of love can analysis expose according to Soler?` is supportable. `Should someone leave their partner according to Lacan?` is not answered by these sources as a general rule. The corpus can explain demand, fantasy, transference, symptom and jouissance; it cannot replace case-specific ethical, practical or safety judgement. [Primary source: Lacan, *Ecrits*, `Direction of the Treatment`. Interpretive source: Soler, Ch. 17.]

Finally, whenever a Notebook answer returns a memorable sentence such as `love is giving what one does not have`, `there is no sexual relationship`, or `a woman is a symptom`, the next research move should be to ask for the exact work, period and immediate conceptual problem. These formulas are useful retrieval anchors precisely because they condense a structure. Detached from their source, however, they are also the points at which Lacanian thought most easily turns into aphoristic folklore. [Primary sources: Lacan, Seminar VIII; Seminar XX. Interpretive source: Soler, Ch. 16.]


## Retrieval warning on aphorisms

This file deliberately uses Lacan's aphorisms as navigation points rather than self-sufficient conclusions. A statement about love should be read together with the surrounding register: mirror identification, demand and desire, transference, object *a*, sexuation, or sinthome. The same English word `love` can therefore perform different conceptual work in different periods. When two passages appear contradictory, first test chronology and register before treating the contradiction as an editorial problem. [Primary base: Lacan, *Ecrits* and Seminars VIII, X, XI, XX, XXIII.]

The same rule applies to interpreters. Fink often clarifies Lacan by making implicit distinctions explicit; Soler often develops Lacanian propositions into clinical and social consequences. Those are valuable additions precisely because they are additions. Notebook retrieval should preserve their names whenever the interpretation goes beyond a directly verified primary passage. [Interpretive sources: Fink, *The Lacanian Subject* and `Freud and Lacan on Love`; Soler, *What Lacan Said about Women*.]


## Final synthesis boundary

The governing boundary of this file is that a relationship is never reduced to a single Lacanian function. The same partner may support imaginary identification, be addressed as an Other, carry an *agalma*, support object *a* in fantasy, become implicated in a symptom, and participate in a durable social pact. These functions can coexist without being equivalent. A research answer that selects one of them should therefore state why that register is relevant and what source period authorises it. [Primary base: Lacan, *Ecrits* and Seminars VIII, X, XI, XX, XXIII; interpretive sources: Fink and Soler.]

This layered approach is the file's main methodological conclusion. Love is neither demystified into one mechanism nor protected from analysis as an ineffable exception. It is treated as a changing set of relations among lack, signifier, desire, knowledge, body, jouissance, symptom and another subject. [Sources: cross-source synthesis established in Sections 1-21.]

CORPUS_ID: 41

# Sexuation, Jouissance, and the Sexual Non-Relation

## Source and edition note

This file is a cross-source analytic synthesis, not a transcription of a single work. Its principal anchor is Jacques Lacan's *Seminar XX: Encore* (1972-1973), in the Bruce Fink English edition loaded in this project. Because that source is scan-heavy, formula pages and passages on sexuation were checked against rendered page images rather than reconstructed from memory. Earlier Lacanian coordinates are supplied by *Écrits*, especially “The Signification of the Phallus” and “Guiding Remarks for a Convention on Female Sexuality,” and by targeted material from Seminars X, XVI, and XVII. Colette Soler and Bruce Fink are treated as major Lacanian interpreters; Alenka Zupančič is treated as a post-Lacanian philosophical development. The *Compendium of Lacanian Terms* is used only as a reference check.

The source hierarchy matters. A proposition in Seminar XX is not made equivalent to Fink's exposition of it, Soler's reconstruction of it, or Zupančič's philosophical extension. When the later sources reorganize Lacan's material, the reorganization is attributed. Translation differences are treated as analytically relevant rather than silently normalized.

## Coverage of this file

The file follows the movement from the earlier Lacanian phallus and the signifier of sexual difference toward the late teaching's logic of sexuation, the division between phallic and Other/feminine jouissance, and the thesis that there is no writable sexual relation. It also treats the consequences of that thesis for love, knowledge, language and *lalangue*, the body, and the divergent readings offered by Soler, Fink, and Zupančič.

The central caution is that Lacan's formulas of sexuation are not a contemporary identity taxonomy. They are logical propositions about positions with respect to the phallic function. They do not straightforwardly sort empirical persons by anatomy, self-identification, sexual orientation, social role, or personality type.


## 1. Conceptual chronology: from phallus to sexuation

Lacan's account of sexual difference does not begin with the four formulas written in *Encore*. The formulas condense a trajectory in which the phallus first appears as a privileged signifier within the dialectic of desire, then becomes formalized as a function, while jouissance, the body, the Other, knowledge, and impossibility increasingly reorganize the problem. Reading the later formulas without that chronology invites two symmetrical errors: reducing them to an elaborate version of Freud's anatomy-centered account, or treating them as a free-floating logical theory detached from the earlier problem of desire and castration.

In “The Signification of the Phallus,” Lacan's decisive move is to sever the phallus from a simple equation with the penis. The phallus is treated as a signifier, and specifically as a privileged signifier in the articulation of desire, lack, and the subject's relation to the Other. The bodily organ can be drawn into this economy, but it is not identical with the function the phallus performs. The subject encounters desire through the fact that the Other's demand and attention are not complete, and the phallus names, within the symbolic economy, what is at stake in that lack. Sexual position is therefore approached through signification rather than as a direct reading of anatomy. [Source: Lacan, *Écrits*, “The Signification of the Phallus,” printed English pp.575-584.]

“Guiding Remarks for a Convention on Female Sexuality” already shows why the problem cannot be solved by simply mapping female anatomy onto a feminine essence. Lacan reviews the analytic difficulties around feminine sexuality, including the inadequacy of straightforward developmental and biological explanations and the limits of a simple clitoral/vaginal opposition. The essay does not yet contain the mature formulas of sexuation, but it establishes a problem that the later teaching will sharpen: the feminine position cannot be derived from anatomy alone, and the available phallic terms do not exhaust the question of feminine jouissance. [Source: Lacan, *Écrits*, “Guiding Remarks for a Convention on Female Sexuality,” printed English pp.610-620.]

Seminar X complicates the phallic organization by developing object *a*, anxiety, and a more precise distinction between lack and the object that causes desire. This matters for sexuation because the later formulas will not simply set “man” and “woman” opposite one another. The masculine side's lower schema, for example, directs the barred subject toward object *a*, while the feminine side is articulated through a different relation to Φ and to the signifier of the barred Other. The later table presupposes the earlier differentiation between the signifier of lack, the cause of desire, and the subject produced by the signifier. [Source: Lacan, Seminar X, targeted material on object *a*, lack, phallus, and sexual difference.]

Seminar XVI introduces *plus-de-jouir*, surplus-jouissance, by drawing on Marx's surplus value and by placing jouissance in a more explicit relation to discourse, loss, and the Other. This does not yet yield the formulas of sexuation, but it shifts the frame. Jouissance is no longer adequately understood as a simple quantity that a subject possesses or loses. It is produced, displaced, extracted, and circulated through signifying structures. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XVI, *From an Other to the other*, Gallagher/private-use translation; translation status noted.]

Seminar XVII continues that shift by formalizing discourse as a structure in which S1, S2, the barred subject, and object *a* occupy different places. Knowledge is no longer treated as neutral representation. It has a structural relation to mastery, truth, production, and jouissance. This is crucial background for *Encore*, where sexual difference is formulated alongside questions of knowledge, writing, the body, and a jouissance that cannot be reduced to what is symbolically mastered. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XVII, *The Other Side of Psychoanalysis*, targeted discourse/knowledge/jouissance material.]

By Seminar XX, the problem is stated as a problem of writing and impossibility. Lacan's claim that there is no sexual relation does not mean that there is no intercourse, no attachment, or no couple. It means that no formula writes a complementary relation between “the sexes” as two terms completing one another. The sexuation table instead gives two logically different ways of being situated with respect to the phallic function. The table's asymmetry is not an imperfection to be repaired; it is the point. The masculine and feminine sides are not mirror images, and their lower lines indicate different relations to object *a*, Φ, and S(Ā). [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, especially Chs. V-VII; formula table visually verified at scan PDF p.45 / printed p.78.]

This chronology changes the meaning of familiar terms. “Castration” increasingly names a structural limitation of jouissance rather than an anatomical threat. “Phallus” names a signifier and later a function rather than an organ. “Sexual difference” becomes a logical difference in modes of inscription rather than a list of empirical traits. “Woman” in the formula “The Woman does not exist” does not mean women do not exist; it means there is no universal signifier that gathers “Woman” into a closed essence corresponding symmetrically to a universal Man. “Not-all” does not mean partially or incompletely feminine. It describes a relation to universality and to the phallic function.

Fink's Chapter 8 in *The Lacanian Subject* is useful precisely because it reconstructs this trajectory pedagogically. He connects castration to loss of jouissance, the phallus to the signifier of desire and lack, and the formulas of sexuation to the absence of a direct complementary relation between the sexes. But his exposition remains an interpretation and simplification of the primary material. [Source: Bruce Fink, *The Lacanian Subject*, Ch.8, “There's No Such Thing as a Sexual Relationship.”]

Soler makes the historical movement explicit in a different way. Her reading stresses that the 1972 formulas do not simply restate Freud's account of masculinity and femininity. They produce a logical differentiation in which the feminine side is “not-all” in the phallic function and in which another, supplementary jouissance can be named without being converted into a positive feminine essence. [Source: Colette Soler, *What Lacan Said about Women*, sections on sexuation and jouissances.]

Zupančič takes the late Lacanian problem further into philosophy. She reads the non-relation not as a contingent failure between two pre-existing sexes but as an antagonism internal to the symbolic field itself. Her claim that sexual division is a problem in ontology is therefore a post-Lacanian development, not a sentence to be attributed to Lacan verbatim. [Source: Alenka Zupančič, *What Is Sex?*, Chs.2-3, especially “Sexual Division, a Problem in Ontology.”]

### 1.1 From a problem of developmental femininity to a problem of logical inscription

The change from the 1950s to Seminar XX can be stated more precisely. In the earlier analytic controversies Lacan inherits a field dominated by questions such as whether femininity is primary or secondary, how the girl relates to the mother and father, what becomes of the phallic phase, and whether vaginal sexuality represents a developmental endpoint. “Guiding Remarks” intervenes in that field by showing how unstable the available developmental answers are. Lacan does not simply choose one developmental school. He shifts the level of the question toward desire, signifier, jouissance, and the partner. [Source: Lacan, *Écrits*, “Guiding Remarks for a Convention on Female Sexuality.”]

The later logic of sexuation can be read as the culmination of that displacement. Instead of asking which developmental path produces the true woman, Seminar XX asks how positions can be written with respect to a function and why no universal relation between those positions can be written. The transition is therefore not merely a change of terminology. It is a change in the kind of problem being posed.

### 1.2 From desire of the Other to lack in the Other

Earlier Lacan repeatedly formulates desire as desire of the Other. This means both that desire is mediated by the Other and that the subject's question concerns what the Other desires. The graph of desire eventually formalizes the point that the Other itself lacks a final signifier, written S(Ā). [Source: Lacan, *Écrits*, “The Subversion of the Subject.”]

Seminar XX reuses this architecture in the lower feminine schema. The relation to S(Ā) is not a new mystical symbol added from nowhere. It draws on the earlier proposition that the Other is barred and cannot guarantee a complete symbolic order. The mature feminine position is therefore linked to a problem already central to Lacan's theory of the subject: there is no Other of the Other that could supply the missing guarantee.

### 1.3 From object relation to object a

The early psychoanalytic vocabulary of “object relations” often suggests a relation between a subject and a whole person who serves as object. Lacan progressively breaks with that picture. Object *a* is not the partner as whole person but a partial remainder and cause of desire. By Seminar X it becomes central to anxiety and fantasy; by Seminar XX it appears in the lower sexuation table as the term toward which the barred subject is directed. [Sources: Lacan, Seminar X; Seminar XX, Ch. VII.]

This trajectory is decisive for the non-relation. If the libidinal partner of the divided subject is mediated by object *a*, then sexual desire is structurally organized around a partial cause rather than direct access to a complementary whole. The later formula does not abolish interpersonal relation; it explains why the relation is never equivalent to a relation between two complete sexed substances.

### 1.4 From structural linguistics to the materiality of lalangue

The 1950s Lacan often emphasizes the autonomy of the signifier and the structural laws of metaphor and metonymy. By Seminar XX, this linguistic orientation is modified by the concept of *lalangue*. The signifier is not only a differential unit in a structure; it is something sounded, repeated, enjoyed, and inscribed in a body. [Sources: Lacan, *Écrits*, “The Instance of the Letter”; Seminar XX, later chapters.]

This development matters for sexuality because it makes the gap between sex and biological program more radical. A speaking body is marked by accidental sounds and signifiers that no universal sexual grammar coordinates. The sexual non-relation is therefore accompanied by singular ways of enjoying language itself.

### 1.5 Continuity and discontinuity

It would be misleading to describe Lacan's teaching as either one unchanging system or a sequence of disconnected theories. The phallus, lack, Other, desire, body, and jouissance persist, but their relations change. The late formulas use earlier notation while altering the conceptual field in which it operates. [Source: chronological comparison across *Écrits* and Seminars X, XVI, XVII, XX.]

For research purposes, this means a statement about “Lacan's view” should normally identify a period. The phallus of the 1958 essay and Φ in the 1972 formulas are connected, but not interchangeable without explanation. Likewise, jouissance in the ethics seminar, surplus-jouissance in Seminar XVI, discourse-produced jouissance in XVII, and the differentiated jouissances of XX form a development rather than a single fixed definition.


## 2. Phallus and phallic function

### 2.1 The phallus is not the penis

The first control distinction is elementary but indispensable: in Lacan, the phallus is not simply the anatomical penis. The organ can become one support of phallic imagery and signification, but Lacan's technical use concerns the phallus as signifier. In “The Signification of the Phallus,” its importance lies in the way it organizes desire, lack, and the relation to the Other. Treating the phallus as an anatomical object collapses symbolic function back into biology and makes the later logic of sexuation unintelligible. [Source: Lacan, *Écrits*, “The Signification of the Phallus,” pp.575-584.]

The phallus is also not object *a*. Fink usefully sharpens this distinction: object *a* is the real cause of desire, not reducible to a signifier, whereas the phallus functions as the signifier of desire and lack. That distinction prevents two common conflations. First, the desired object in an empirical relationship is not automatically “the phallus.” Second, the cause of desire is not exhausted by the signifier through which desire is symbolically articulated. [Source: Fink, *The Lacanian Subject*, Ch.8, section “The Phallus and the Phallic Function.”]

### 2.2 From lack to function

Lacan's earlier formulations locate the phallus in a structure of lack. Desire arises where need has been mediated by demand and where the Other cannot provide a final guarantee or complete satisfaction. The phallus gives symbolic form to this lack rather than abolishing it. This is why the phallus can be described as the signifier of what is missing in the Other's desire and, by extension, of the subject's own lack in relation to the signifier. [Source: Lacan, *Écrits*, “The Signification of the Phallus”; “The Subversion of the Subject.”]

Fink reconstructs the transition to the “phallic function” by tying it to the alienating effect of language itself. On this reading, the phallic function institutes or formalizes the loss that accompanies entry into language. Castration is therefore not primarily an event in which an organ is threatened or removed. It is a structural renunciation or limitation of jouissance. Because speaking beings are constituted through the signifier, neither anatomical sex is exempt from this limitation. [Source: Fink, *The Lacanian Subject*, Ch.8, sections “Castration” and “The Phallus and the Phallic Function.”]

This is consistent with the trajectory from Seminars XVI and XVII to XX. Jouissance is not simply a natural reserve that language leaves untouched. The signifier both produces and limits modes of enjoyment. Surplus-jouissance names, in one phase of this development, the remainder or product generated by a structure of loss. By *Encore*, the phallic function becomes the principal logical operator through which sexuation is written. [Sources: Lacan, Seminar XVI; Seminar XVII; Seminar XX.]

### 2.3 Φ in the formulas of sexuation

In Seminar XX, Φ is not a shorthand for “male sexuality.” It designates the phallic function against which the four quantified propositions are written. On the left, the masculine side is structured by a universal submission to Φ together with an exception: there exists an *x* for whom the phallic function is negated. On the right, the feminine side denies that there is such an exception and also denies that the universal “all x” can be asserted without qualification. The logic is asymmetric. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Ch. VII, formula table at printed p.78.]

This is why it is misleading to translate the table into the claim that men “have the phallus” and women “lack it.” Both sides are defined through the phallic function, but not in the same logical way. The feminine side is not outside the phallic function as if it belonged to a separate pre-symbolic realm. Rather, the formula “not-all” means that the feminine position cannot be wholly enclosed by the universal quantification of the phallic function. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Chs. VI-VII; Soler, *What Lacan Said about Women*, sections on the not-all.]

### 2.4 Being and having are not stable gender properties

Earlier Lacanian discussion sometimes uses the language of “being” and “having” the phallus. This can be useful if retained within the dialectic of desire, semblance, and the Other, but it is easily turned into a stereotype. “Being the phallus” does not mean being biologically female or being passive; “having the phallus” does not mean possessing a penis or embodying social masculinity. These positions concern how desire is staged in relation to what the Other is presumed to lack. [Source: Lacan, *Écrits*, “The Signification of the Phallus.”]

The late formulas displace this vocabulary by formalizing positions in relation to Φ. They should therefore not be read as a final metaphysical proof that one sex naturally “has” and the other naturally “is.” The formulas are better understood as an attempt to write the structural impasse without turning sexual difference into a positive essence.

### 2.5 Phallic function and castration

The phallic function is inseparable from castration because it marks that jouissance is limited by the signifier. Fink's explanatory formulation is that castration requires the speaking being to give up some jouissance. In Lacan's own later economy, this loss is not simply a deprivation. It is constitutive: a speaking body becomes capable of specifically human forms of desire and enjoyment precisely because jouissance is mediated, cut, displaced, and supplemented by language. [Source: Fink, *The Lacanian Subject*, Ch.8; Lacan, Seminars XVI-XX.]

The phallic function therefore has a double character. It limits jouissance, but it also makes possible a determinate, symbolically organized jouissance. “Phallic jouissance” is not merely sexual pleasure located in a male organ. It is a mode of jouissance structured by the signifier and by the limit that Φ names. The later distinction between phallic and Other/feminine jouissance can only be understood if this positive-productive side of limitation is kept in view. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Chs. I, VI-VII.]

### 2.6 The signifier of desire and the problem of the Other

The phallus becomes especially important because desire is never presented by Lacan as a transparent inner appetite. Desire is mediated by the Other: by the language in which the subject asks, by the demand addressed to caregivers and partners, and by the question of what the Other wants. In this structure, the phallus is not the answer to desire but a signifier around which the lack in the Other's desire is articulated. [Source: Lacan, *Écrits*, “The Signification of the Phallus” and “The Subversion of the Subject.”]

This distinction matters for sexual difference because a subject does not first possess a complete sexed identity and then enter a relationship. The subject is constituted through a field of signifiers in which the desire of the Other is already at stake. The phallus names a privileged point in that field. Sexuation therefore concerns how a speaking being is positioned within a pre-existing symbolic problem, not how an isolated organism expresses an inner sexual essence.

### 2.7 Why the phallus remains controversial

Even once it is distinguished from the penis, the privileged place of the phallus remains theoretically and politically contentious. Lacan's own argument gives several reasons for the phallus's symbolic salience, including visibility, erection/detumescence, and its role in the imagery of generation. Fink emphasizes that such explanations are contingent and anthropological rather than structurally necessary in the strongest sense. [Source: Fink, *The Lacanian Subject*, Ch.8, drawing on Lacan.]

This contingency creates an interpretive tension. If the phallus is merely the contingent privileged signifier of desire in a particular symbolic order, one can ask whether another signifier could occupy that function. If, however, the formulas of sexuation depend irreducibly on Φ, the term appears more structurally entrenched. The project sources do not resolve that tension into a single uncontested answer. File 42 therefore keeps the distinction between the formal function Φ and historical explanations for why “phallus” names that function.

### 2.8 Phallic function and the signifier's cut

The phallic function can also be approached from the way the signifier introduces discontinuity into bodily life. Needs do not pass directly into satisfaction; they are articulated in demand, delayed, substituted, interpreted, and caught in the Other's response. Satisfaction becomes detachable from biological function. Eating can become oral demand, withholding can become anal satisfaction, looking can become scopic enjoyment, and speech itself can carry jouissance. [Sources: Lacan, Seminars X, XVI-XX.]

In that sense, the phallic function belongs to a broader operation of cut. It formalizes the fact that speaking beings do not enjoy as self-contained organisms. Enjoyment is marked by loss and by a signifier that can stand in for what is absent. This is why the later formulas can treat Φ as a function applying to variables rather than as an anatomical emblem.

### 2.9 Castration without deficit psychology

Because castration names a structural limit, it should not be translated into a deficit psychology in which one class of people possesses something and another lacks it. The phrase “lack of the phallus” becomes misleading when heard as a literal missing organ or as lower status. In Lacan's logic, lack is constitutive of the subject as such. The masculine side is not the non-lacking side; it is the side on which submission to Φ is universalized through an exception. [Sources: Lacan, Seminar XX; Fink, Ch.8.]

Likewise, the feminine not-all is not a compensatory privilege awarded to a deprived subject. It designates another logical relation to the same function. The whole architecture therefore resists a simple hierarchy of possession and deprivation even though its terminology remains historically marked by the phallus.


## 3. Sexual difference and identity categories

### 3.1 Sexuation is not an identity taxonomy

Lacan's mature term *sexuation* concerns the way a speaking being is situated with respect to the phallic function. This should not be silently equated with biological sex, gender identity, sexual orientation, social role, or personality style. These empirical and social categories may intersect with psychoanalytic material, but the formulas themselves do not provide a one-to-one classification of them. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Ch. VII; Fink, *The Lacanian Subject*, Ch.8.]

This distinction is especially important because Seminar XX retains historically gendered terms, including “man,” “woman,” masculine, and feminine. A source-faithful reading should neither erase those terms nor convert them into contemporary categories the source does not contain. The analytical task is instead to specify the level at which Lacan is working. The table proposes logical positions. It does not offer a demographic survey of men and women, a theory of chromosomes, or a clinical questionnaire for assigning a person's “true” sexuation.

Fink explicitly notes that Lacan's theory aims to move beyond defining sexual difference biologically. He emphasizes that anatomy, social definitions of masculinity and femininity, and object choice can diverge. His pedagogical conclusion is that the psychoanalytic distinction must therefore be sought at another level, namely the subject's relation to language, castration, and jouissance. [Source: Fink, *The Lacanian Subject*, Ch.8, section “Distinguishing between the Sexes.”]

Soler makes a related distinction between identification and sexuation. Identification concerns the signifiers and images through which a subject is represented and recognizes itself; sexuation concerns a logical position with respect to the phallic function and jouissance. A person can accumulate masculine or feminine identifications without those identifications providing a transparent map of the position at stake in the formulas. [Source: Soler, *What Lacan Said about Women*, sections on “Identification or Sexuation” and the logic of sexuation.]

### 3.2 Anatomy matters, but it does not decide the formulas by itself

To say that sexuation is not reducible to anatomy is not to claim that bodies are irrelevant. Psychoanalysis starts from embodied subjects, from sexual difference as encountered in bodies, from reproduction, excitation, organ pleasure, fantasy, and the meanings attached to anatomy. What Lacan contests is the claim that anatomy supplies a complete symbolic program for sexual relation. The body does not come with a ready-made signifier that writes a complementary relation between two sexes. [Sources: Lacan, *Écrits*, “Guiding Remarks for a Convention on Female Sexuality”; Seminar XX.]

This is one reason the sexual non-relation is not an anti-biological thesis. It does not deny reproductive dimorphism or the existence of bodily differences. It says that no biological difference automatically supplies the symbolic formula that would tell a speaking being how “man” relates to “woman” as complementary terms. The speaking being must pass through language, fantasy, identification, and jouissance.

### 3.3 Sexual orientation is not the same thing as sexuation

Object choice does not determine sexuation in any simple way. A subject who desires men is not thereby on the feminine side of the formulas, and a subject who desires women is not thereby on the masculine side. Lacan's lower schema itself indicates why. The masculine side is not defined by a particular empirical partner but by the barred subject's relation to object *a*. The feminine side is not defined by attraction to men but by a relation to Φ and to S(Ā), the signifier of the barred Other. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Ch. VII, formula table.]

Fink stresses this point in a different vocabulary: biological sex, social gender, and choice of sexual object can diverge. His reconstruction is useful because it blocks the familiar assumption that Lacan is merely redescribing heterosexual complementarity. In fact, the thesis of the non-relation is precisely a rejection of such complementarity. [Source: Fink, *The Lacanian Subject*, Ch.8.]

### 3.4 “Masculine” and “feminine” are asymmetric logical positions

The formulas do not describe two symmetrical substances. The masculine side is organized by universality and exception: all are submitted to the phallic function, but the universal is supported by an exception in which the function is negated. The feminine side denies the exception and is therefore not closed as a universal totality. This is the logical background of the “not-all.” [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Ch. VII.]

The asymmetry matters. If the two sides were simple opposites, they could be defined by reciprocal complementarity. But Lacan's formulas refuse this. The masculine side is not the inverse image of the feminine side. The different lower relations in the table reinforce that structural non-equivalence.

### 3.5 The statement “The Woman does not exist”

Lacan's barred “Woman” in the sexuation table does not mean that empirical women do not exist. It means that “Woman” cannot be written as a universal essence that would gather every woman under one complete signifier. The absence is at the level of the universal signifier, not at the level of persons. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Chs. VI-VII.]

Soler repeatedly emphasizes this point. To say that Woman does not exist is to refuse an essence of femininity that could be positively stated for all women. The feminine position is “not-all” in the phallic function, which means precisely that no closed universal predicate captures it. [Source: Soler, *What Lacan Said about Women*, sections on the manifestations of the not-all and the logical formulas of sexuation.]

This should not be converted into the claim that women are more mysterious, less rational, less linguistic, or outside culture. Those are stereotypes, not implications of the formulas. Nor does “not-all” grant a privileged moral or spiritual status to women. The formulas concern the logic of inscription and jouissance, not a hierarchy of human worth.

### 3.6 Contemporary categories and historical caution

The vocabulary of Seminar XX predates contemporary distinctions among sex assigned at birth, gender identity, gender expression, and sexual orientation. A responsible corpus synthesis should therefore do two things at once. It should preserve Lacan's actual terminology and logical claims, and it should resist presenting those claims as if they directly answered contemporary classificatory questions they were not designed to answer.

Nothing in the formulas provides a simple psychoanalytic test for whether a contemporary person is “really” a man, woman, non-binary person, heterosexual, homosexual, bisexual, or otherwise. Such an application would move beyond the evidence base of the source. The useful contribution of sexuation to contemporary questions is narrower: it challenges the assumption that sexual identity can be fully grounded in a natural symbolic complementarity already written into the body or the social order.


## 4. The formulas of sexuation

### 4.1 Source location and transcription status

The canonical source for the formula block in this corpus is the table Lacan writes at the beginning of Chapter VII, “A Love Letter,” in Seminar XX. In the project scan it appears on PDF page 45, corresponding to printed page 78. The page was visually inspected at high resolution. The table has two columns, conventionally called the masculine side on the left and the feminine side on the right. The upper half contains four quantified propositions involving Φx. The lower half places the barred subject, Φ, object *a*, S(Ā), and barred Woman in asymmetric relations. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Ch. VII, printed p.78; visual verification against project scan.]

Because plain Markdown cannot reproduce the original typography perfectly, the following is a normalized transcription with explanatory notes. It is not a replacement for the scanned page.

### 4.2 Upper formulas

Masculine side, left column:

```text
∃x  ¬Φx
∀x   Φx
```

Feminine side, right column:

```text
¬∃x ¬Φx
¬∀x  Φx
```

The precise typography in the scan uses overbars/negations placed over the function or quantified proposition. The logical content is as follows.

On the masculine side, the lower proposition states that every *x* is submitted to the phallic function. The upper proposition posits an exception: there exists an *x* for whom the phallic function is negated. Lacan links this exception structure to the logic of the father and to the way a universal can be founded by an exception. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Ch. VII, immediately following the formula table.]

On the feminine side, there is no such exception. No *x* can be posited outside the phallic function in the form required to found a symmetrical universal. Yet the universal proposition is itself negated: one cannot write “all x are Φx” as the exhaustive definition of the feminine position. This is the “not-all.” [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Ch. VII.]

### 4.3 Why the formulas are not four empirical statements

The quantified letters do not denote a sampled population of biological men and women. The formulas are mathemes intended to formalize positions in relation to a function. Reading `∀x Φx` as a statistical statement such as “every biological man is phallic” and `¬∀x Φx` as “some women are not phallic” misses Lacan's logic. The right side does not posit an empirical subset of women outside the function. It says that the feminine side cannot be totalized under the universal quantifier, even though there is no constitutive exception of the masculine type. [Sources: Lacan, Seminar XX, Chs. VI-VII; Soler, *What Lacan Said about Women*; Fink, *The Lacanian Subject*, Ch.8.]

The paradox is deliberate. If there is no exception, classical intuition might suggest that the universal should be stronger. Lacan uses the formulas to articulate the opposite: the absence of an exception prevents the closure of a universal totality of “Woman.” This is one reason later interpreters have compared the formulas to antinomies rather than to ordinary demographic classification. [Sources: Seminar XX; Zupančič, *What Is Sex?*, section on sexual division and the formulas of sexuation.]

### 4.4 Lower schema

The lower half of Lacan's table is equally important because it prevents the upper formulas from being treated as pure logic detached from desire and jouissance.

On the masculine side appear the barred subject, `$` or S̄, and Φ. The barred subject's arrow crosses toward object *a* on the feminine side. This indicates that the masculine subject does not relate directly to “Woman” as a complementary whole. The partner is approached through the object-cause of desire, object *a*, and through fantasy. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Ch. VII, formula table.]

On the feminine side appears barred Woman, conventionally written `Ⱥ` or `La̸` depending on the transcription system. The feminine side has a relation toward Φ and another toward S(Ā), the signifier of the lack in the Other. Lacan's placement is not symmetrical with the masculine side's relation to object *a*. It is this asymmetry that supports the distinction between phallic jouissance and the supplementary jouissance Lacan associates with the feminine not-all. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Chs. VI-VII.]

A compact descriptive transcription is therefore:

```text
MASCULINE SIDE                    FEMININE SIDE

∃x ¬Φx                            ¬∃x ¬Φx
∀x  Φx                            ¬∀x  Φx

S̄  ───────────────→  a           S(Ā)
                                    ↑
Φ  ←────────────────  Woman̸      Woman̸ also relates to Φ
```

This diagram is schematic. The original spatial arrangement on printed page 78 remains the source of record.

### 4.5 The exception and the universal

Lacan's masculine universal is structurally tied to an exception. The logic resembles the way a law can be constituted by positing an outside that founds its inside. In the sexual formulas, the exception is not a living empirical “superman” exempt from castration. It is the logical exception required by the structure of the universal. Lacan connects this with the Freudian father of the primal horde, the one imagined to have access to all women and to be exempt from the prohibition imposed on the sons. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Ch. VII; background in Freud as taken up by Lacan.]

The feminine side is constructed differently. It does not gain consistency by postulating an exception outside Φ. Hence it cannot form a closed universal in the same way. The barred Woman in the lower table corresponds to this impossibility of writing a universal feminine essence. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Ch. VII.]

### 4.6 The formulas as writing of an impossibility

The formulas do not write the sexual relation. They write the logical conditions under which no complementary relation can be written. Each side is formalized in relation to Φ, not directly in relation to the other side. The lower arrows likewise route the positions through object *a*, Φ, and S(Ā). The table therefore demonstrates the non-relation rather than repairing it. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Ch. VII.]

Fink's exposition makes this point explicitly: if the sexes could be defined directly as reciprocal inverses, one could write an equation of complementarity. Lacan instead defines each side separately with respect to a third term, and the partners indicated by the formulas are not symmetrical. [Source: Fink, *The Lacanian Subject*, Ch.8.]

### 4.7 Limits of formal paraphrase

A matheme is not exhausted by its prose explanation. The formulas condense several different Lacanian registers: quantification, exception, castration, the phallic function, fantasy, object *a*, the barred Other, and two modes of jouissance. Any plain-language rendering therefore risks over-stabilizing what the formula keeps structurally related.

For that reason this corpus uses the formulas as a navigation device rather than as a four-line definition of men and women. Interpretive statements are tied back to the primary table and attributed to specific commentators when they go beyond what Lacan explicitly says on the page.

### 4.8 Reading the table vertically and horizontally

The table should not be read only row by row. Vertically, each side combines upper logical propositions with lower libidinal relations. Horizontally, the two sides are deliberately non-isomorphic. This double reading prevents a common simplification in which the four quantified formulas are treated as the whole theory of sexuation. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Ch. VII.]

On the left, the universal and exception structure culminates in the barred subject's relation to object *a*. On the right, the absence of a founding exception and the not-all culminate in a divided relation toward Φ and S(Ā). The table thus ties formal logic to fantasy, desire, the Other, and jouissance. It is not an abstract appendix detached from clinical theory.

### 4.9 The barred subject and object a

The barred subject in Lacan's notation is the subject divided by the signifier. Its relation to object *a* condenses the fantasy formula `$ ◊ a` familiar from earlier Lacanian teaching. Seminar XX's lower schema does not rewrite the whole fantasy formula, but it relies on that architecture: the subject's desire reaches the partner through an object-cause that is partial and structurally produced. [Sources: Lacan, *Écrits*, “The Subversion of the Subject”; Seminar X; Seminar XX, Ch. VII.]

This point matters because it shows that the masculine side's difficulty is not simply “fear of intimacy” or a social tendency to objectify. The mediation by *a* belongs to Lacan's general theory of desire. Clinical manifestations vary widely, and the formula does not license a stereotype about individual men.

### 4.10 The feminine arrows and divided relation

The lower feminine side is often more difficult to read because barred Woman is connected both to Φ and to S(Ā). The relation to Φ shows that the feminine position is not simply outside phallic jouissance. The relation to S(Ā) indicates a supplementary orientation toward the lack in the Other, correlated with the Other jouissance discussed in the preceding chapter of Seminar XX. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Chs. VI-VII.]

The two arrows should therefore not be turned into two personality styles. They write a divided libidinal structure. A feminine subject can participate in phallic desire and jouissance while also being not-all contained by that organization.

### 4.11 The formula as anti-complementarity

The table's most important negative result is that there is no arrow joining “Man” and “Woman” as whole terms. No upper proposition says that one sex is the logical negation or inverse of the other. No lower arrow establishes a complete reciprocal exchange. The table is thus an anti-complementarity machine: it writes the detours through which relation occurs and, in doing so, writes the absence of the direct relation. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Ch. VII.]

This is why attempts to convert the formulas into “masculine energy” and “feminine energy” betray their structure. Such language usually reinstates a yin/yang complementarity that Lacan's table is designed to undo.

### 4.12 Formula, matheme, and transmission

Lacan's use of mathemes aims at a form of transmission less dependent on intuitive psychological meaning. A matheme can be repeated, inspected, and manipulated even when its interpretation remains difficult. Yet the formulas of sexuation show the limit of this ambition as well: the notation is transmissible, but its meaning remains contested because it condenses multiple registers. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX; *Compendium*, entry “Mathème” as secondary reference.]

For corpus purposes, the matheme therefore functions as an anchor. Interpretations are accepted only insofar as they remain compatible with the primary arrangement and are explicitly sourced. The diagram cannot be replaced by a slogan.


## 5. Universal, exception, and not-all

### 5.1 “All” on the masculine side

The masculine side of the formulas couples a universal proposition with an exception. The formula conventionally rendered `∀x Φx` says that every x on this side is subject to the phallic function. But the universal is supported by `∃x ¬Φx`, the existence of an x for whom the function does not apply. The logical architecture is therefore not “men are completely phallic” in an empirical sense. It is that a universal class is constituted through an exception. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Ch. VII.]

Lacan links the exception to the paternal function and to the mythic father who would not be castrated. The point is structural, not biographical. No ordinary father or man occupies the position as an empirical exemption from language or castration. The exception gives the universal its consistency as a logical structure. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Ch. VII; Fink, *The Lacanian Subject*, Ch.8.]

### 5.2 The feminine side has no founding exception

The right-hand upper formula denies that there exists an x for whom Φ is negated in the exceptional way. There is no equivalent feminine exception that would stand outside the function and found a universal set of Woman. This is why Lacan cannot simply write a second symmetrical universal opposite the masculine universal. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Ch. VII.]

The second feminine formula is consequently not an ordinary universal. Lacan writes the universal quantifier with negation, giving the famous “not-all.” The feminine position is related to the phallic function, but the function does not totalize it. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Chs. VI-VII.]

### 5.3 What “not-all” does not mean

“Not-all” does not mean “not entirely,” as if a woman were 70 percent phallic and 30 percent outside it. It does not mean “not every woman,” as a simple statistical claim that some women are phallic and some are not. It does not mean that women are defective members of a universal class whose complete form is male. And it does not mean that feminine subjects are outside language, law, or castration. [Sources: Lacan, Seminar XX; Soler, *What Lacan Said about Women*.]

The not-all is a statement about the failure of totalization. The feminine position cannot be enclosed by the universal quantifier in the same way as the masculine side. This makes possible Lacan's claim that there is a supplementary jouissance beyond phallic jouissance, but it does not place a positive substance called “the feminine” outside the symbolic order.

### 5.4 Not-all and “The Woman does not exist”

The nonexistence at stake in “The Woman does not exist” is the nonexistence of a universal signifier of Woman. Empirical women exist; feminine identifications exist; social categories of women exist. What is barred is the possibility of writing a universal essence that would tell us what Woman is in the singular and for all. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Chs. VI-VII.]

Soler's formulation is useful here: femininity cannot be made into a closed set by a predicate that says what all women are. This is why the feminine position has to be approached one by one rather than through a universal essence. Her phrase “not-all” preserves the logical point and resists both biological essentialism and an idealized mystique of feminine alterity. [Source: Soler, *What Lacan Said about Women*, sections on the not-all and sexuation.]

### 5.5 Not-all is compatible with relation to Φ

A recurrent misunderstanding is to treat feminine not-all as a clean exit from the phallic function. Lacan's lower schema contradicts that reading: barred Woman has a relation to Φ. The feminine side is not a non-phallic territory. Its specificity lies in not being exhaustively determined by phallic jouissance. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Ch. VII.]

This is also why Lacan calls the additional jouissance “supplementary” rather than simply complementary. A supplement adds without completing a pre-existing whole. The term fits the not-all because there is no closed totality to which the second jouissance could be added as the missing half.

### 5.6 The one-by-one consequence

If there is no universal Woman, the feminine side cannot be deduced from a general essence. Clinically and conceptually, this pushes Lacanian discourse toward singularity: how a particular subject relates to Φ, to the Other, to love, to the body, and to jouissance cannot be read off from membership in a universal category. [Source: Seminar XX; Soler, *What Lacan Said about Women*.]

This consequence should not be romanticized. “One by one” does not mean women are uniquely individual whereas men are generic or interchangeable. It means that the logic of the feminine side does not close under the universal in the same way as the masculine side. The masculine subject is no less singular as a subject; the difference concerns the formalization of sexuation.

### 5.7 Zupančič's extension: two ways of dealing with contradiction

Zupančič develops the formulas as two ways of configuring the contradiction internal to the signifying order. On her reading, sexual difference is not a difference between two positive substances but the division generated around a constitutive negativity. The two sides are not two independent identities; they are two formalizations of the same impossibility. [Source: Zupančič, *What Is Sex?*, “Sexual Division, a Problem in Ontology.”]

This is a powerful post-Lacanian interpretation because it explains why the formulas should not be turned into a binary of complementary traits. But it is an interpretation. The corpus therefore uses it to clarify the stakes of the formulas while preserving the distinction between Zupančič's ontological language and Lacan's primary seminar.

### 5.8 The paternal exception and myth

The masculine exception is often connected to Freud's myth of the primal father in *Totem and Taboo*: a father imagined as having access to all women and exempt from the prohibition that binds the sons. Lacan's use of the myth is not a historical claim that such a father existed. The myth supplies a figure for the logical exception that supports the universal law. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Ch. VII, with Freudian background.]

This makes the exception a useful example of the relation between myth and logic in Lacan. The myth dramatizes what the formula condenses. Once written as `∃x ¬Φx`, the exceptional father no longer needs to be understood as a psychological image of an actual patriarch. He functions as the logical point outside the universal that allows the universal to be constituted.

### 5.9 Why absence of exception changes the whole structure

The feminine side does not simply delete the exceptional father and leave the rest unchanged. If no exception exists, the universal itself cannot be founded in the same way. Hence the second formula negates universal totalization. The two right-hand formulas belong together. Reading only `¬∃x ¬Φx` and concluding “everyone is phallic” misses the second half of the structure. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Ch. VII.]

This is the conceptual core of the not-all. It is not a weaker universal but a different logical regime. No outside exception founds a closed inside, so the set cannot be totalized in the same way.

### 5.10 Not-all and singularity

The absence of a universal Woman is why Lacanian commentators often emphasize “one by one.” The phrase should be used carefully. It does not mean that feminine subjects are uniquely individual whereas masculine subjects are mass-produced by the universal. Rather, no universal predicate can define the feminine position in advance. Each case must therefore be approached without presuming a complete feminine essence. [Source: Soler, *What Lacan Said about Women*.]

Clinically, this is consistent with psychoanalysis's general resistance to treating diagnostic or sexuated categories as complete descriptions of a person. The formal structure can orient listening, but singular signifiers, fantasies, symptoms, and modes of jouissance remain decisive.

### 5.11 Not-all and the barred Other

The not-all also resonates with Lacan's broader thesis that the Other is not complete. S(Ā) marks the lack of a signifier in the Other that would guarantee the symbolic order. The feminine lower arrow toward S(Ā) therefore links sexual non-totalization to the non-totality of the Other itself. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Ch. VII; earlier notation in *Écrits*.]

This is a structural link, not the claim that women are “closer to the unconscious” in an essentialist sense. The feminine position is formalized through a relation to the barred Other; it does not confer mystical access to a complete truth beyond language.


## 6. Phallic jouissance and Other/feminine jouissance

### 6.1 Jouissance is not ordinary pleasure

The French term *jouissance* resists a single English equivalent. It can suggest enjoyment, use, possession, and sexual climax, but Lacan's technical use is broader and often more paradoxical. Jouissance names an enjoyment that may exceed pleasure, including modes of satisfaction that are costly, repetitive, painful, or bound to the signifier in ways the subject does not consciously choose. The *Compendium* correctly warns that translating it simply as pleasure loses this excess, though the Compendium remains a secondary source. [Source: *A Compendium of Lacanian Terms*, entry “Jouissance.”]

The distinction is already visible in the movement from Freud's pleasure principle to Lacan's account of enjoyment beyond homeostatic pleasure. By Seminar XX, Lacan is no longer asking simply where pleasure is located. He is differentiating modes of jouissance according to their relation to the phallic function, the body, language, and the Other. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Chs. I, V-VII.]

### 6.2 Phallic jouissance

Phallic jouissance is jouissance structured by the phallic function. It is not “male orgasm.” Nor is it a possession reserved for men. The term designates a mode of enjoyment that passes through the signifier and its limit. Its very localization is linked to castration: something is cut, prohibited, or made impossible, and enjoyment returns in the constrained form permitted by that symbolic organization. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX; Fink, *The Lacanian Subject*, Ch.8.]

This helps explain why phallic jouissance is simultaneously possible and limited. The phallic function does not merely suppress enjoyment. It produces a determinate way of enjoying by imposing a structure on the speaking body. Language is therefore not external to sexuality. The signifier carves the body, marks zones, creates prohibitions and repetitions, and organizes what can count as satisfaction. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX; Soler, “The Body in the Teaching of Jacques Lacan.”]

The masculine side of the sexuation table is wholly inscribed under Φ, but this does not mean that men enjoy more fully. On the contrary, the universal submission to Φ means that the masculine position is entirely caught in the phallic regime. The barred subject's lower arrow toward object *a* further indicates that the partner is mediated by fantasy and the object-cause of desire rather than accessed as a complementary Other. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Ch. VII, formula table.]

### 6.3 Other or feminine jouissance

In Chapter VI of Seminar XX, “God and Woman's Jouissance,” Lacan develops the possibility of a jouissance supplementary to phallic jouissance. He associates it with the feminine not-all and with a relation to S(Ā), the signifier of the lack in the Other. This jouissance is “other” not because it belongs to a second complete symbolic system but because it is not exhausted by the phallic function. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Ch. VI.]

The term “feminine jouissance” is therefore potentially misleading if heard as a biological property of women. The logic is positional. Lacan speaks through the historically gendered vocabulary of masculine and feminine, but the conceptual claim is that the feminine position is not wholly enclosed by phallic jouissance. The formulas do not authorize the empirical statement that all women experience a special second pleasure, or that no man can have any relation to what Lacan is trying to formalize. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX; Soler, *What Lacan Said about Women*.]

### 6.4 Supplementary, not complementary

Lacan's preferred logic is supplementation rather than complementarity. If Other jouissance simply completed phallic jouissance, the two would form the very sexual relation that Lacan says cannot be written. A complementary pair would imply that the missing half of one side is supplied by the other. “Supplementary” is more exact: another jouissance can occur without closing the structure into a whole. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Chs. VI-VII.]

Soler stresses this point. The feminine position can be related to phallic jouissance and yet not be entirely contained by it. The additional jouissance is therefore not a separate female substance. It is an effect of the not-all. [Source: Soler, *What Lacan Said about Women*, sections on jouissances and sexuation.]

### 6.5 The mystical references

Lacan's discussion of feminine jouissance famously invokes mystics. This should be read carefully. He does not thereby define feminine jouissance as ordinary religious feeling or establish theology as empirical proof. The mystics function as witnesses to an experience that is said to occur but resists full articulation in knowledge. Lacan's point is less “mysticism explains women” than that certain writings testify to an enjoyment that exceeds the speaker's available signifiers. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Ch. VI, “God and Woman's Jouissance.”]

The relation to God in this chapter is likewise not a straightforward theological doctrine. Lacan uses the God of certain philosophical and mystical traditions to interrogate the locus of the Other, existence, and jouissance. The argumentative setting is one in which “the Other” is already barred: no complete Other guarantees the symbolic order. The mystical reference therefore cannot be reduced to an assertion that feminine sexuality has privileged access to a metaphysical being.

### 6.6 One knows that it happens, not what it is

A recurrent feature of Lacan's presentation is the distinction between the occurrence of the supplementary jouissance and the possibility of saying what it is. The feminine subject may be said to have a relation to this jouissance without thereby possessing a knowledge that could define it exhaustively. This connects the sexuation problem to the seminar's broader treatment of knowledge, truth, and *lalangue*. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Chs. VI-VIII.]

This is another reason not to translate feminine jouissance directly into orgasm. Orgasm can be described physiologically and can occur across bodies and identities. Lacan is theorizing a relation between enjoyment and the limits of symbolic knowledge. Sexual physiology may be involved, but the concept is not reducible to a physiological event.

### 6.7 Jouissance and the non-relation

The two modes of jouissance do not provide two halves that fit together. Phallic jouissance is already mediated by object *a* and fantasy. Other jouissance does not supply a matching term that would complete the masculine side. The distinction therefore intensifies the non-relation. It describes different modes of enjoyment without yielding a formula of reciprocal complementarity. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Chs. VI-VII.]

Zupančič radicalizes this point by arguing that sexuality is organized around a constitutive contradiction rather than around the failure to reach a pre-existing harmony. On that reading, jouissance is not noise interfering with an otherwise possible relation. The disturbance belongs to the structure itself. [Source: Zupančič, *What Is Sex?*, Chs.2-3.]

### 6.8 Phallic jouissance and the One

Seminar XX repeatedly links phallic jouissance to the logic of the One rather than to a relation between two complementary terms. Enjoyment is localized, counted, repeated, and tied to the signifier's operation. This helps explain why the sexual relation cannot be reconstructed by simply joining two individual jouissances together. Each speaking being's enjoyment is organized through its own relation to signifiers, fantasy, and bodily events. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, opening chapters and Chs. V-VII.]

The logic of the One also clarifies Lacan's provocative separation of jouissance from the partner. A sexual act can involve another person while the subject's jouissance remains structured by fantasy and by signifying coordinates that are not reducible to reciprocity. This is not a moral accusation of selfishness. It is a structural proposition about the way enjoyment is organized.

### 6.9 Surplus-jouissance as a bridge to Seminar XX

Seminar XVI's *plus-de-jouir* provides an important transition. Lacan borrows from Marx's surplus value to describe a remainder of enjoyment produced by the very operation that imposes loss. This undermines any simple economy in which castration removes enjoyment and leaves only deprivation. The signifier produces new forms of satisfaction even as it limits others. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XVI, *From an Other to the other*.]

By Seminar XVII, surplus-jouissance is integrated into discourse theory through object *a*. Knowledge, mastery, truth, and production are organized around a remainder that cannot be assimilated into the system. Seminar XX can then approach sexuation without imagining that phallic limitation leaves a pure natural jouissance intact behind it. The “beyond” of phallic jouissance is not a return to pre-symbolic wholeness. [Sources: Lacan, Seminars XVI-XVII; Seminar XX.]

### 6.10 Why Other jouissance cannot be a missing object

If Other jouissance were a definable object that could be acquired, it would function like another item in the economy of desire. Lacan's presentation resists this.
