The supplementary jouissance is said to be experienced without being fully known, and its relation to S(Ā) ties it to the lack in the Other rather than to a hidden positive signifier. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Ch. VI.]

This distinguishes Other jouissance from object *a*. Object *a* is the cause around which desire turns and the remainder generated by the signifying structure. Other jouissance is a mode of enjoyment correlated with the feminine not-all. Conflating them would destroy the distinctions encoded in the lower table.

### 6.11 The temptation to idealize feminine jouissance

Because Lacan invokes mystics and a jouissance beyond the phallus, later readers can idealize the feminine position as more authentic, embodied, spiritual, or free. The primary source does not justify such a hierarchy. The supplementary jouissance is not presented as morally superior to phallic jouissance, nor as a path of liberation that anyone should seek. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Ch. VI.]

Soler's insistence on the not-all helps block this idealization. The feminine position is not a complete outside from which the phallic order can be judged. It remains related to Φ. The additional jouissance is therefore an excess of totalization, not an unalienated natural state. [Source: Soler, *What Lacan Said about Women*.]

### 6.12 Enjoyment, knowledge, and testimony

The mystical passages also raise a methodological question: how does psychoanalysis know about a jouissance that resists articulation? Lacan's answer is not experimental measurement. He relies on testimony, speech, writing, and the clinical fact that subjects can indicate an experience without supplying a complete knowledge of it. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Ch. VI.]

This is consistent with psychoanalysis more generally, where an experience can have effects before it is fully symbolized. Yet the epistemic modesty is important. A concept built around what cannot be fully said must not be turned into a positive catalogue of properties. The less the source specifies, the more cautious the synthesis must remain.


## 7. Body and jouissance

### 7.1 The speaking body

Lacan's theory of jouissance is a theory of the body, but not of the biological body taken as a self-sufficient organism. The body relevant to psychoanalysis is a body marked by images, signifiers, drives, prohibitions, fantasies, and forms of satisfaction. Seminar XX repeatedly treats jouissance as something that happens to and through bodies while refusing to derive its structure from physiology alone. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, especially Chs. I, V-VII.]

Soler's “The Body in the Teaching of Jacques Lacan” provides a useful historical reconstruction. She distinguishes the living organism from the psychoanalytic body and argues that, across Lacan's teaching, a body is not simply given at birth as the relevant analytic object. In the early work, the mirror image helps unify the organism into a body; with the linguistic turn, the signifier is said to introduce its own divisions and inscriptions. Later Lacan places greater emphasis on the real of the body and on jouissance. [Source: Colette Soler, “The Body in the Teaching of Jacques Lacan.”]

### 7.2 The signifier does not merely describe the body

If language only named an already complete biological body, the sexual problem could in principle be solved by more accurate classification. Lacan's stronger claim is that the signifier participates in producing the body as a field of erogenous zones, prohibitions, symptoms, and enjoyments. The body is not untouched matter subsequently described by words. Speaking changes the economy of satisfaction itself. [Sources: Lacan, Seminars XVI-XX; Soler, “The Body in the Teaching of Jacques Lacan.”]

Fink's account of castration develops this as a loss of jouissance required by entry into language. The lost enjoyment is not simply annihilated; forms of jouissance return through speech, fantasy, symptom, and surplus-jouissance. His discussion of the “jouissance packed in the signifier” is an expository way of showing that language can itself become a site of enjoyment. [Source: Fink, *The Lacanian Subject*, Ch.8.]

### 7.3 Phallic jouissance is bodily without being reducible to anatomy

Phallic jouissance is often connected to bodily sexuality, but the adjective “phallic” indicates symbolic organization rather than anatomical ownership. The phallic function gives a limit and form to enjoyment. Consequently, a body with or without a penis cannot be read directly as the bearer of one side of the formulas. The relevant question is how jouissance is structured in relation to Φ, the signifier, and the subject's fantasy. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Chs. VI-VII.]

This also clarifies why Lacan can maintain that jouissance is of the body while insisting that sexuation is not a simple biological classification. The body is real, imaginary, and symbolic at once in the psychoanalytic field. Anatomical differences may be symbolized, fantasized, or made the support of identifications, but their psychoanalytic function depends on the signifying network in which they are caught.

### 7.4 Other jouissance and bodily experience

Other or feminine jouissance is not a disembodied spirituality. The mystical examples in Seminar XX are invoked precisely because they testify to an experienced enjoyment, something said to occur in the body while exceeding complete articulation. The limit is epistemic and symbolic, not a departure from embodiment. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Ch. VI.]

Soler's reconstruction is useful here because it rejects the idea that the real body is merely the nervous system or a biological substrate to which psychoanalysis adds meanings. For Lacan, what is real in the body concerns points where symbolic organization reaches an impasse and where jouissance cannot be wholly integrated into coherent meaning. [Source: Soler, “The Body in the Teaching of Jacques Lacan.”]

### 7.5 The body is not a guarantee of sexual relation

Bodies can couple, reproduce, touch, and experience pleasure. None of this, for Lacan, yields the missing symbolic formula of the sexual relation. This is one of the sharpest distinctions in Seminar XX. The non-relation is not disproved by the empirical existence of sexual acts because the claim concerns the impossibility of writing a complementary signifying relation between sexuated positions. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Chs. I, V-VII.]

The body therefore supplies both the site of jouissance and the evidence that no purely symbolic account can close sexuality into a harmonious system. Enjoyment is experienced, but its organization is partial, asymmetrical, and bound to a signifier that never writes the two as One.

### 7.6 Lalangue and the enjoyed body

The later Seminar XX emphasis on *lalangue* radicalizes the bodily side of language. *Lalangue* is not language as a clean system of communication. It points to the material resonances, equivocations, sounds, and deposits of speech through which a speaking body is affected and enjoys. This gives a different sense to the proposition that the unconscious is structured like a language: the later teaching increasingly stresses not only structure and meaning but the enjoyment carried by signifying material. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, later chapters on knowledge, signifier, and *lalangue*.]

This does not erase the symbolic. Rather, it makes the symbolic inseparable from material effects on the body. A word can wound, excite, repeat, stick, or organize a symptom beyond what the speaker intends to communicate. The body's jouissance is therefore neither prelinguistic nature nor pure meaning.


## 8. “There is no sexual relationship”

### 8.1 What the formula denies

Lacan's `il n'y a pas de rapport sexuel` is one of the most easily trivialized formulas in his teaching. It does not mean that people do not have sex, that couples do not exist, that relationships are always unhappy, or that mutual attachment is impossible. Lacan is not reporting a sociological failure rate. The formula concerns what can be written as a relation between sexuated positions. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Chs. I, V-VII.]

Fink highlights the ambiguity of the French *rapport*: it can refer to relation, relationship, ratio, proportion, or even, in the plural, sexual intercourse. His expository point is that Lacan's claim operates in the more abstract register. Nothing can be written that would define “man” and “woman” as complementary reciprocal terms whose combination yields a complete One. [Source: Fink, *The Lacanian Subject*, Ch.8, “There's No Such Thing as a Sexual Relationship.”]

### 8.2 No direct complementary equation

If sexual difference were a relation of simple opposition, it could be written by defining each term through the other. If one side were active and the other passive, or if the two were inverse functions that added up to a whole, the relation would be formalizable. Fink illustrates the kind of impossible dream involved with the idea of two curves that could be combined into a complete identity. Lacan's formulas refuse precisely that sort of complementarity. [Source: Fink, *The Lacanian Subject*, Ch.8.]

The sexuation table defines each side with respect to Φ rather than directly with respect to the other sex. The lower arrows also route desire and jouissance through object *a*, Φ, and S(Ā). The “partner” is never simply the other sex as such. Fantasy, object-cause, signification, and different modes of jouissance intervene. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Ch. VII.]

### 8.3 The non-relation is structural, not a failure of communication

A common therapeutic reading turns the formula into the banal proposition that partners misunderstand one another. Lacan's claim is stronger. Better communication may alter a relationship, but it does not create the missing sexual formula. The impossibility is not caused by insufficient empathy, poor vocabulary, or incomplete information. It is built into the way sexual difference is articulated in the signifier. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX.]

This is why Seminar XX links the sexual non-relation to writing and logic. Lacan asks what can be written, what does not cease not to be written, and what can occur contingently even where no necessary relation is inscribed. The language of necessity, impossibility, contingency, and possibility is not decorative. It marks the shift from a psychology of failed pairing to a logic of non-inscription. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, later chapters.]

### 8.4 There is relation, but not “the” sexual relation

The formula does not eliminate relations. Subjects relate to fantasy, object *a*, the phallus, the Other's desire, the signifier, their own symptoms, and the partner as filtered through these structures. Couples therefore exist through mediations. The non-relation means there is no direct formula that bypasses those mediations and establishes two sexuated terms as natural complements. [Sources: Lacan, Seminar XX; Fink, *The Lacanian Subject*, Ch.8.]

This is a useful way to understand why psychoanalysis can say both that there is no sexual relation and that love, desire, jealousy, fantasy, and sexual bonds are clinically decisive. These phenomena are not evidence against the non-relation; they are among the ways speaking beings construct bonds where no pre-written complementary formula exists.

### 8.5 The non-relation and impossibility

In Lacan's modal vocabulary, the impossible is associated with what “does not cease not to be written.” The sexual relation belongs to this register of impossibility. The important point is not that a particular couple fails to write it, but that the relation as such has no necessary inscription in the symbolic order. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, later chapters on writing and modality.]

This formulation also prevents the non-relation from being read pessimistically as a verdict that intimacy is futile. Impossibility is a structural category. Contingent encounters, attachments, sexual acts, and loves can occur. What they do not receive is the guarantee of a universal formula saying how the two sexes fit together.

### 8.6 Zupančič: non-relation as constitutive negativity

Zupančič's post-Lacanian development sharpens the point by arguing that the non-relation is not a gap between two already constituted positive terms. It is constitutive of the terms themselves. Sexual difference emerges around a negativity or contradiction that the symbolic order cannot absorb into a harmonious totality. [Source: Zupančič, *What Is Sex?*, Ch.2 “The Quandary of the Relation” and Ch.3.]

On this reading, sexuality is not messy because an otherwise complete relation has been disturbed. The apparent mess is one of the ways the impossibility is negotiated. Fantasies, identities, social norms, and sexual scenarios do not merely obscure a natural relation beneath them; they organize the field produced by the absence of such a relation.

### 8.7 Social relations and sexual non-relation

Zupančič further extends non-relation beyond sexuality by examining how social orders can exploit or cover over constitutive antagonisms. This should not be conflated with Lacan's sexual formula. Her point is analogical and philosophical: structures often attempt to present a fundamental non-relation as if it were a harmonious relation. [Source: Zupančič, *What Is Sex?*, Ch.2.]

For File 42, the value of that extension is diagnostic. It warns against turning the sexual non-relation into a positive identity doctrine. The non-relation is not another relation. It is the failure of a relation to write itself as a complete formula.

### 8.8 Writing versus lived experience

The language of writing in Seminar XX can appear remote from lived sexuality, but the distinction is deliberate. Lacan does not infer the non-relation from a survey of disappointing relationships. He asks whether a relation can be formally inscribed. Lived experience then shows the consequences of that absence through fantasy, repeated choices, symptoms, demands, and inventions. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX.]

This means that empirical counterexamples do not refute the thesis. A stable couple cannot prove that the sexual relation exists in Lacan's technical sense, just as a conflictual couple cannot by itself prove that it does not. The level of the thesis is structural, not statistical.

### 8.9 The non-relation and fantasy

Fantasy can be understood as one of the devices through which the subject gives consistency to desire where no sexual formula exists. The fantasy `$ ◊ a` stages a relation between the divided subject and object *a*. It provides a repeatable frame for desire, but that frame is not the sexual relation. It is a substitute construction organized around a partial object. [Sources: Lacan, *Écrits*, “The Subversion of the Subject”; Seminar X; Seminar XX.]

This is why a subject's fantasy can make a partner seem to occupy an intelligible place while the underlying relation remains non-complementary. Different partners can be recruited into similar fantasy structures, and the same partner can be experienced differently as the fantasy shifts.

### 8.10 The non-relation and symptom

Although File 45 treats symptom and sinthome in depth, File 42 needs one bridge: where no sexual relation is written, symptoms can participate in how a subject makes a relation possible. A symptom can organize enjoyment, limit anxiety, and provide a repetitive solution to the impasse of desire. Later Lacanian formulations about a partner as symptom or sinthome develop this point further, but they should not be retrojected wholesale into Seminar XX. [Source: Seminar XX as background; later Lacan reserved for File 45.]

The relevant implication here is modest: the absence of a universal relation does not produce a void devoid of structure. Subjects construct substitutes, and those substitutes can be highly stable.

### 8.11 Non-relation and heterosexual normativity

Because the formulas use masculine/feminine sides, they can be misread as a sophisticated justification of heterosexual complementarity. The thesis of non-relation works in the opposite direction. It denies that heterosexual difference carries an intrinsic symbolic formula of complementarity. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX.]

At the same time, Lacan's terminology and examples emerge from a historically heteronormative psychoanalytic context. A contemporary reader should neither ignore that context nor conclude that the formulas straightforwardly validate modern identity categories. The theoretically defensible point is narrower: no object choice, including heterosexual object choice, by itself writes the missing relation.

### 8.12 Non-relation and the real

The sexual non-relation belongs to Lacan's concept of the Real insofar as the Real is approached through impossibility. The Real is not a hidden natural sexual relation lying underneath language. It is encountered at the point where symbolic writing fails. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX.]

This is one reason Zupančič's contradiction reading is powerful. She rejects the image of a positive Real substance behind the symbolic and instead treats the Real as the contradiction or impasse internal to symbolization. Sexual non-relation then becomes a privileged name for that impasse. [Source: Zupančič, *What Is Sex?*, Chs.3-4.]

### 8.13 Why “non-relation” is not nihilism

A structure without a guaranteed relation is not a structure without meaning or bonds. Lacan's theory shifts the question from “what is the correct natural relation?” to “how is a bond constructed where no such relation is written?” This opens analytic attention to fantasy, symptom, love, demand, identification, and singular modes of jouissance. [Source: Seminar XX; interpretive synthesis grounded in Fink and Soler.]

The non-relation therefore has a double function. Negatively, it blocks fantasies of total complementarity. Positively, it explains why so much psychic work is devoted to making relations, sustaining them, repairing them, and giving them meaning.


## 9. Love and the sexual non-relation

### 9.1 Love does not abolish the non-relation

Seminar XX places love beside the non-relation without presenting love as a technical fix for it. Love can establish a bond, an address, a contingency, or a way of making something hold between subjects, but it does not write the missing complementary formula between sexuated positions. The distinction is essential. If love completed the two sides into a One, the thesis of the non-relation would collapse. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Chs. I, V-VII and later modal formulations.]

The opening sessions repeatedly connect jouissance, love, and the impossibility of a sexual relation. Lacan can say that love aims at being, that love has a relation to the signifier, and that the sexes do not form a relation writable as such. These are not three unrelated observations. Love enters precisely where there is no natural symbolic complementarity that would make a bond automatic. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Chs. I-IV.]

### 9.2 The signifier and the address of love

Earlier Lacan had already defined love in paradoxical terms, including the well-known formulation about giving what one does not have. The point is not exchange of a possessed object. Love is structured by lack and address. One speaks to an Other from a position in which neither subject nor Other is complete. [Source: Lacan, Seminar VIII; cross-reference in *Écrits* translator notes and later Seminar XX.]

Seminar XX radicalizes this by placing love in a field where the Other itself is barred. There is no final signifier that guarantees the sexual relation. Love therefore cannot rely on a complete symbolic program. It must be spoken and enacted through contingent signifiers, identifications, promises, names, and encounters.

### 9.3 Love as contingency

The later modal vocabulary gives a precise way to understand this. What is impossible as a necessary sexual relation can nevertheless encounter moments in which something “stops not being written.” Love is often read in this vicinity: not as the discovery of a pre-existing harmony but as a contingent inscription that makes a bond possible without transforming contingency into necessity. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, later chapters on modality, knowledge, and love.]

This is why a love relation can be real and consequential without disproving the sexual non-relation. A contingent bond can hold, fail, be renewed, or become symptomatically indispensable. None of that yields a universal formula that would specify in advance how “man” and “woman” fit.

### 9.4 Love and knowledge

Love is also linked to knowledge in Seminar XX, but not because love is simply accurate knowledge of another person. The beloved is never fully transparent to the lover. The subject supposes, imagines, reads, and addresses an Other whose desire and jouissance remain partly opaque. The non-relation thus persists inside love as a limit to knowledge.

The same limit prevents psychoanalysis from turning love into a program of total mutual understanding. Analysis can alter fantasies, demands, and the subject's position toward desire, but it does not produce a complete knowledge of sexual complementarity. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX; Fink and Soler as interpretive context.]

### 9.5 Soler on feminine love

Soler develops Lacan's material on feminine love in a more clinically articulated direction. She distinguishes femininity from hysteria and examines how demand for love, desire, and jouissance can be differently combined. Her discussion should not be collapsed into a universal rule that “women love in one way.” It is a reconstruction of structural positions and recurring analytic configurations. [Source: Soler, *What Lacan Said about Women*, chapters on feminine love and later sections on love.]

Soler's broader point is compatible with the not-all: feminine position cannot be reduced to a single universal predicate, including a universal predicate about how women love. Where she discusses demands for signs of love, partner relations, or marriage, the claims remain tied to clinical structures and to Lacan's logic, not to a general psychology of women.

### 9.6 Love, object a, and the partner

The masculine lower schema in the sexuation table directs the barred subject toward object *a*. This means that the empirical partner is encountered through a fantasy structure and through the object-cause of desire. The person loved is not reducible to object *a*, but the subject's libidinal relation to the partner is mediated by it. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Ch. VII.]

This also explains why love and desire need not coincide smoothly. A subject can love where desire falters, desire where love is absent, or organize both around different fantasy coordinates. The non-relation does not force these divisions, but it removes the fantasy that a natural complementarity should automatically align them.

### 9.7 Love as response, supplement, or semblance

Different Lacanian commentators use different terms to describe love's relation to the non-relation: response, supplement, encounter, contingency, semblance. These should not be treated as interchangeable slogans. “Supplement” emphasizes that love adds something without completing a whole. “Contingency” emphasizes that the bond occurs without necessity. “Semblance” emphasizes the signifying and imaginary forms through which the bond is sustained. [Sources: Seminar XX; Soler; Fink.]

The common denominator is negative: love does not erase castration, abolish object *a*, supply a final signifier of Woman, or write the sexual relation. Its importance lies precisely in what can be constructed despite that absence.

### 9.8 Love and the illusion of knowing the partner

The non-relation helps explain why love so readily becomes entangled with knowledge. Lovers ask what they are for the other, what the other wants, whether the other's words are true, and what hidden signification belongs to gestures and silences. The Other's desire becomes an interpretive problem. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX; earlier background in the graph of desire.]

No accumulation of information resolves that question completely because the Other is itself divided. The partner does not possess a final transparent knowledge of their own desire that could simply be communicated. This does not make communication worthless; it places a structural limit on the fantasy that enough disclosure could eliminate opacity.

### 9.9 Love, demand, and the sign of love

Earlier Lacanian theory distinguishes demand from desire and treats demand as extending beyond specific needs into demand for love. A requested act can therefore carry a demand for a sign that the subject matters to the Other. Sexual and romantic relationships often become saturated with this signifying dimension: the issue is not only what was done but what the act “means” about love. [Source: Lacan, *Écrits*, “The Signification of the Phallus”; Seminar VIII as background.]

Seminar XX does not erase this structure. It places it within a field where no sexual relation guarantees what a sign of love should be. The same act can therefore fail as a sign for one subject and function powerfully for another. The contingency of love is inseparable from this absence of a universal code.

### 9.10 Love and the One

Lacan's late discussions repeatedly interrogate the One. Love often imagines two becoming one, but the seminar's logic resists a fusion into a completed whole. The bond can be intense without abolishing division. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX.]

This makes the ordinary metaphor of “two halves” theoretically misleading. If each subject were a half whose true partner restored wholeness, sexual relation would already be written as complementarity. Lacan's love is more paradoxical: it can create a powerful experience of relation precisely where structure provides no such guarantee.

### 9.11 The practical limit of the theory of love

The source material does not provide a normative model of a good relationship. It does not rank relationship structures, prescribe a communication method, or specify how often partners should have sex, express affection, or resolve conflict. Such questions belong to other clinical or empirical frameworks. [Source limit of Seminar XX and approved interpretive sources.]

What Lacanian theory contributes is a structural warning against demanding from the partner what no partner can structurally provide: a final signifier of one's being, complete knowledge of desire, or the missing complementary term that would close lack. This is an interpretive implication, not a behavioral prescription.


## 10. Knowledge, truth, language, and lalangue

### 10.1 Knowledge is not neutral information

The French *savoir* in Lacan's later teaching should not be reduced to factual information or conscious understanding. Seminars XVI and XVII had already formalized knowledge, S2, as structurally implicated in discourse and jouissance. Knowledge can occupy a place in the master's discourse, be produced by the hysteric's discourse, and be separated from truth. Seminar XX inherits this transformation. [Sources: Lacan, Seminars XVI-XVII.]

This matters for sexuation because the absence of a sexual relation is not simply a gap in human knowledge that future science could fill. The missing formula is not an undiscovered fact waiting to be learned. Lacan treats it as a structural impossibility of inscription. More knowledge about bodies, reproduction, or psychology does not automatically produce the relation that cannot be written. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX.]

### 10.2 Truth is not total knowledge

Lacan's long-standing distinction between truth and knowledge remains active. Truth can only be half-said; no complete metalanguage closes the symbolic order. The barred Other, S(Ā), in the sexuation table indexes this absence of a complete guarantee. The feminine side's relation to S(Ā) therefore connects sexuation to the incompleteness of the Other, not to a secret total knowledge possessed by women. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Ch. VII.]

This prevents another romantic misunderstanding: Other jouissance is not a hidden wisdom that could complete rational knowledge. Lacan's insistence that it is experienced without being fully known points toward a limit of articulation, not toward possession of a superior esoteric doctrine.

### 10.3 Language and the impossibility of sexual complementarity

The sexual non-relation is inseparable from language because speaking beings do not encounter sexuality outside the signifier. The very categories through which bodies, partners, acts, and identities become meaningful are signifying. Yet language does not provide a final signifier that writes the two sexes as complementary. The signifying system generates the problem it cannot solve by a final relation. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX; earlier background in *Écrits*.]

Fink's exposition frames this as the fact that the sexes are defined separately in relation to a third term rather than directly in relation to one another. The phallus is a signifier of desire and lack, object *a* remains the cause of desire, and the Other itself is lacking. The relation cannot therefore be closed by adding more signifiers. [Source: Fink, *The Lacanian Subject*, Ch.8.]

### 10.4 From language to lalangue

*Lalangue* names something different from language understood as an orderly system for communication. It points to the materiality of speech: sound, homophony, equivocation, repetition, family idiom, accidental association, and the singular way words strike a speaking body. In Seminar XX this shift is central to the relation between the unconscious and jouissance. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, later chapters on knowledge, signifier, and *lalangue*.]

A language can be described grammatically and semantically; *lalangue* emphasizes what exceeds clean communication. The same sounds can support several meanings, a phrase can carry an enjoyment independent of its informational content, and a signifier can become attached to a bodily satisfaction. The unconscious is therefore not only an interpretable system of meanings but also a deposit of enjoyed signifying material.

### 10.5 Jouissance of speech

The move toward *lalangue* helps explain how jouissance can be connected to knowledge without becoming knowledge about jouissance. A body can enjoy a signifier, a rhythm, a pun, or a repeated phrase without the subject possessing a transparent account of why. Speech can carry satisfaction in excess of what it communicates. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX; Fink, *The Lacanian Subject*, discussion of jouissance in language.]

This also qualifies the idea that analysis is simply translation of unconscious content into conscious knowledge. Interpretation can produce knowledge, but it also intervenes in signifying material and in the subject's mode of enjoyment. The later Lacanian emphasis on letter, equivocation, and *lalangue* pushes beyond a hermeneutics in which every symptom would dissolve into a complete meaning.

### 10.6 Knowledge and the formulas

The sexuation formulas are themselves an experiment in writing. Lacan uses formal notation because ordinary discourse tends to re-import images of masculine and feminine complementarity. The matheme attempts to isolate logical relations while simultaneously demonstrating a limit: what can be written are the positions with respect to Φ and their associated relations, not a formula of the sexual relation itself. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Ch. VII.]

The formulas therefore have a paradoxical epistemic role. They increase formal knowledge of sexuation while formalizing the impossibility of a complete sexual knowledge. The more precise the writing becomes, the more sharply it writes the absence of the relation rather than filling it in.

### 10.7 Later Lacan and the body of knowledge

The arc from Seminars XVI-XVII to XX also changes the meaning of “knowledge in the Other.” Knowledge can function without being owned by a conscious subject. Discourses, institutions, scientific formulas, and language itself can carry knowledge. Yet the Other is barred: no total body of knowledge guarantees itself. Sexual difference becomes a privileged site where this non-totality is exposed. [Sources: Lacan, Seminars XVII and XX.]

Zupančič uses this point philosophically. Her account of sex as contradiction argues that the failure of symbolic closure is not merely an epistemic defect. The inconsistency belongs to the structure being known. This is her post-Lacanian extension of the relation between sex, knowledge, and ontology. [Source: Zupančič, *What Is Sex?*, Ch.3.]

### 10.8 Lalangue versus linguistic structure

Earlier Lacan is frequently summarized through the formula that the unconscious is structured like a language. Seminar XX does not simply abandon that proposition, but it changes the emphasis. Structural linguistics foregrounds differential relations among signifiers; *lalangue* foregrounds the singular material through which those signifiers are heard, enjoyed, and sedimented in a body. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, later chapters.]

This distinction matters for sexuation because no universal grammar can tell a subject how to inhabit sexual difference. The speaking being receives fragments of speech, names, jokes, insults, family sayings, prohibitions, and equivocations. These materials do not form a coherent sexual instruction manual. They become the raw material from which desire and jouissance are organized.

### 10.9 Knowledge in the unconscious

Lacan can speak of unconscious knowledge without implying a hidden little person in the mind who knows the truth. Knowledge is in the chain of signifiers, in repetitions and formations that operate without conscious mastery. The subject can therefore “know” in one sense while not knowing consciously. [Sources: Lacan, Seminars XVII-XX.]

In sexuality this creates a distinctive paradox. Subjects can repeat highly organized libidinal patterns while sincerely declaring that they do not know what they want. The existence of such unconscious knowledge still does not amount to knowledge of a sexual relation. It is knowledge of signifying and jouissance patterns within the absence of a universal formula.

### 10.10 The barred Other and the absence of metalanguage

S(Ā) indicates that the Other lacks a signifier that would close and guarantee the symbolic system. This is related to Lacan's proposition that there is no metalanguage capable of standing outside language and providing its final truth. [Source: *Écrits*, “The Subversion of the Subject”; Seminar XX.]

The sexual non-relation is a privileged consequence. No superior symbolic code can be consulted to determine the complete complementary formula of the sexes. Religious, scientific, social, and romantic discourses can provide scripts, but none occupies the place of an unbarred Other that guarantees the relation.

### 10.11 Science and sex

Seminar XX repeatedly engages scientific formalization. Lacan admires the capacity of mathematical writing to isolate relations that ordinary meaning obscures. Yet sexuation marks a point where formalization writes an impossibility rather than a complete relation. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX.]

This makes the relation between psychoanalysis and science complex. Psychoanalysis does not reject biological or scientific knowledge of sex. It claims that such knowledge does not by itself answer the psychoanalytic problem of sexuation and sexual relation. Chromosomes, hormones, reproductive anatomy, and population patterns can be scientifically described without yielding the missing signifier of complementary sexual relation.

### 10.12 Interpretation and equivocation

The importance of *lalangue* also affects analytic interpretation. If the signifier carries jouissance through sound and equivocation, interpretation cannot be reduced to explaining the correct hidden meaning of a statement. Puns, cuts, repetitions, and homophonies can matter because they touch the material by which the body is affected. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, later chapters.]

This is relevant to File 42 because sexual signifiers are often especially saturated with inherited meanings and enjoyments. Terms for body parts, gendered insults, pet names, family narratives, and sexual labels can operate as more than descriptive language. They can organize jouissance even when the speaker consciously rejects their literal meaning.


## 11. Colette Soler's reading

### 11.1 Soler as interpreter, not primary source

Colette Soler's *What Lacan Said about Women* is one of the most sustained attempts in the project corpus to reconstruct Lacan's changing account of women, femininity, sexuation, love, and jouissance. Its value for File 42 lies in its systematic distinctions. It should nevertheless be read as Soler's interpretation of Lacan rather than as a substitute for Seminar XX. [Source: Soler, *What Lacan Said about Women*.]

### 11.2 Woman, mother, hysteria, femininity

Soler repeatedly resists conflations that would make the feminine position identical with motherhood or hysteria. “Woman” cannot be defined by maternal function, and hysteria is not simply another name for femininity. Hysterical structure can make femininity into a question, stage desire around the Other, or identify with a position from which the question “what is a woman?” is sustained. But that is different from the logical not-all of Seminar XX. [Source: Soler, *What Lacan Said about Women*, early and middle chapters on hysteria and femininity.]

This distinction is crucial because popularized readings of Lacan often move too quickly from clinical categories to sexuation. A hysteric can be anatomically male or female; a feminine position cannot be inferred from a diagnosis; and the sexuation formulas are not a disguised typology of hysteria versus obsession.

### 11.3 The not-all and the impossibility of a universal Woman

Soler treats the formulas of sexuation as a decisive break with attempts to define femininity by a positive universal property. The masculine side gains universal consistency through the exception. The feminine side has no corresponding exception and thus no universal Woman. “The Woman does not exist” names this failure of universalization. [Source: Soler, *What Lacan Said about Women*, sections on the manifestations of the not-all and logical sexuation.]

For Soler, this does not make women outside the phallic function. The not-all means that the feminine position is not wholly contained in it. This nuance protects the concept from two distortions: a phallocentric reading in which woman is merely deficient man, and an anti-phallic romantic reading in which woman inhabits a pure beyond of language.

### 11.4 Two jouissances

Soler gives particular weight to Lacan's distinction between phallic and supplementary jouissance. Phallic jouissance is limited and structured by castration. The supplementary jouissance associated with the feminine not-all does not cancel that first relation. A feminine subject can have a relation to Φ and also to another jouissance. [Source: Soler, *What Lacan Said about Women*, chapters/sections on jouissances.]

This is why Soler's reading is more precise than the slogan “women have another jouissance.” The key word is not possession but relation. The supplementary jouissance is not a female organ function and does not constitute an essence common to all women. It is one consequence of the not-all logic.

### 11.5 Femininity and semblance

Soler also preserves the importance of semblance. Because there is no universal signifier of Woman, femininity cannot simply be displayed as a natural essence. Feminine appearances, identifications, and positions can operate as semblances without being therefore false or superficial. The absence of an essence makes the play of signifiers and appearances structurally important. [Source: Soler, *What Lacan Said about Women*, sections on appearances of sex and feminine position.]

A semblance is not the opposite of reality in the ordinary sense. In Lacan, social and sexual bonds depend on signifiers, images, titles, roles, and conventions that can function even where no final ontological guarantee exists. Soler uses this framework to avoid both biological essentialism and a simple voluntarist account of femininity.

### 11.6 Feminine love

Soler's later discussions of feminine love distinguish demand for love from the supplementary jouissance of the not-all. Love can become particularly important where no signifier provides a complete identity or relation, but this should not be universalized into a claim that women are naturally more loving or dependent. [Source: Soler, *What Lacan Said about Women*, later chapters on feminine love, marriage, and partners.]

Her discussions of partnership also remain subordinate to the sexual non-relation. A partner can function as symptom, support, addressee, or condition of a mode of jouissance, but none of these functions establishes a complementary sexual formula.

### 11.7 What Soler adds

Soler's distinctive contribution is to make the logical formulas clinically and conceptually legible without turning them into a psychology of traits. She tracks how desire, hysteria, motherhood, love, semblance, phallic function, and supplementary jouissance intersect while insisting that they are not synonyms. Her work is therefore particularly useful for policing category errors in readings of Seminar XX.

At the same time, Soler's vocabulary belongs to a later Lacanian tradition. When she systematizes “the manifestations of the not-all” or draws clinical implications from the formulas, those formulations should be cited to Soler rather than retroactively presented as verbatim Lacan.

### 11.8 Identification or sexuation

One of Soler's most useful distinctions is between identification and sexuation. Identification provides names and signifiers through which the subject can answer, provisionally, “who am I?” Sexuation concerns a different question: how is the subject positioned with respect to the phallic function and modes of jouissance? [Source: Soler, *What Lacan Said about Women*, section “Identification or Sexuation.”]

This distinction has practical consequences. A strongly feminine identification does not prove a feminine sexuation, and masculine identification does not prove a masculine sexuation. The same applies to social presentation, occupational role, clothing, and object choice. These can be analytically important without being equivalent to the logical formulas.

Soler's distinction also protects against making sexuation into a consciously chosen identity. The formulas concern unconscious structure and jouissance, not simply the label a subject prefers. At the same time, the theory does not authorize an analyst to dismiss a person's stated identity by claiming access to a hidden “true sex.” The source corpus provides no simple procedure for such an adjudication.

### 11.9 Hysteria and the question of Woman

Soler's treatment of hysteria clarifies another recurrent category error. The hysteric often sustains the question of what the Other desires and may make femininity itself into an enigma. This can produce intense identification with or rivalry around other women, a search for the signifier of femininity, or a position of making oneself the cause of the Other's desire. [Source: Soler, *What Lacan Said about Women*, chapter on hysteria and femininity.]

But the hysterical question “what is a woman?” is not the same as the feminine not-all. Hysteria can remain deeply organized by phallic desire and by the demand for a signifier that would answer the question. The feminine position in Seminar XX instead formalizes the impossibility of a universal signifier of Woman. The distinction is therefore between sustaining the question within the Other and occupying a logical position in which the failure of universalization is constitutive.

### 11.10 Motherhood and the child as object

Soler devotes substantial attention to motherhood because psychoanalytic traditions have repeatedly used maternal function as a substitute definition of femininity. She rejects that substitution. The mother is positioned through desire, demand, and relation to the child; the child can occupy the place of an object in the maternal fantasy. None of this establishes motherhood as the essence of Woman. [Source: Soler, *What Lacan Said about Women*, Part IV on the mother.]

This distinction reinforces the logic of sexuation. Reproductive capacity does not supply the missing universal signifier. A woman can be a mother, a mother can be organized by phallic desire, and maternal jouissance can be analytically important, but the category “mother” does not solve the problem of the feminine position.

### 11.11 Soler on the causes and effects of jouissance

Soler's later chapters examine different modes of jouissance and their subjective consequences. She emphasizes that phallic jouissance is limited, countable, and structured by castration, whereas supplementary jouissance is not organized in the same discrete way. [Source: Soler, *What Lacan Said about Women*, later chapters on jouissances.]

This distinction helps explain why Lacan's additional jouissance cannot simply be measured as “more pleasure.” Its difference is qualitative and structural, not a greater quantity on a common scale. The notion of a supplementary jouissance therefore resists both physiological reduction and ordinary hedonistic comparison.

### 11.12 Soler's use of “absolute Other”

Soler sometimes uses formulations such as the “absolute Other” to describe aspects of the feminine position or the place from which a woman can appear as Other in phallic logic. Such phrases require care because they can sound metaphysical. In her Lacanian framework, “Other” is not a mystical substance or a positive essence of women. It marks a structural alterity generated by the failure of a universal signifier. [Source: Soler, *What Lacan Said about Women*, early chapters on Woman and the Other.]

This is consistent with the barred Woman of Seminar XX. The absence of a universal predicate can make “woman” function as Other within masculine fantasy, but the fantasy of absolute alterity is itself not the same as the logical feminine position.

### 11.13 What Soler prevents File 42 from doing

Soler's work functions as a set of negative controls. It prevents the synthesis from defining femininity by anatomy, motherhood, hysteria, passivity, or a generic demand for love. It prevents the not-all from being turned into a vague celebration of feminine fluidity. It prevents supplementary jouissance from being equated with ordinary sexual pleasure. And it keeps the nonexistence of Woman at the level of universal signification rather than empirical existence. [Source: Soler, *What Lacan Said about Women*.]

These controls make Soler particularly valuable for a research corpus likely to be queried in ordinary language, where the technical distinctions can otherwise collapse into familiar gender stereotypes.


## 12. Bruce Fink as an expository bridge

### 12.1 Why Fink is useful here

Bruce Fink's Chapter 8, “There's No Such Thing as a Sexual Relationship,” in *The Lacanian Subject* provides one of the clearest English-language pedagogical reconstructions of Lacan's sexuation material in the project corpus. It is especially useful for showing how castration, the phallus, the phallic function, the absence of a sexual relation, the formulas of sexuation, and Other jouissance fit into one argumentative sequence. It is not, however, an authorised paraphrase of Seminar XX. File 42 therefore uses Fink as an expository bridge and checks the decisive formal claims against Lacan's primary table. [Source: Fink, *The Lacanian Subject*, Ch.8.]

### 12.2 Castration as renunciation of jouissance

Fink begins by shifting castration away from a literal anatomical threat. Drawing on Lacan's seminars, he formulates castration as the requirement that the speaking being give up or alienate some jouissance. In his reconstruction, entry into language is already a loss: the subject cannot take jouissance wholly “inside” itself, and a part of enjoyment is displaced into the Other, speech, knowledge, and social circulation. [Source: Fink, *The Lacanian Subject*, Ch.8, “Castration.”]

This exposition is useful because it connects the sexual formulas to a more general theory of subject formation. Sexuation does not appear as a special topic added after the theory of the subject is complete. The same signifying operation that constitutes the subject also institutes a loss and a limit. The phallic function later formalizes how that limit is taken up in sexuated positions.

Fink also connects Lacan's loss of jouissance to surplus-jouissance and to Marx's surplus value. The analogy is not identity. The point is structural: a subtraction or loss can generate a remainder that circulates. In the libidinal field, object *a* and surplus-jouissance name different aspects of what remains and returns after the subject's insertion into signifying structure. [Source: Fink, *The Lacanian Subject*, Ch.7-8; Lacan, Seminar XVI as underlying source.]

### 12.3 The phallus as signifier of desire and lack

Fink's exposition of the phallus is particularly helpful for preventing the organ/signifier conflation. He treats the phallus as the signifier of desire and as the signifier of lack. The child encounters the fact that the caretaking Other desires beyond the child; a privileged signifier comes to stand for what draws the Other's desire elsewhere. In Lacan's Western clinical context, that signifier is the phallus. [Source: Fink, *The Lacanian Subject*, Ch.8, “The Phallus and the Phallic Function.”]

Fink explicitly distinguishes the phallus from object *a*. Object *a* is the unsignifiable real cause of desire, whereas the phallus is a signifier of desire. This distinction matters directly to the lower sexuation schema. The barred subject on the masculine side is directed to *a*, not to “Woman” as an immediately available complementary object. The feminine side, in turn, is written in relation to Φ and S(Ā), not as the object that completes the masculine subject. [Sources: Fink, Ch.8; Lacan, Seminar XX, Ch. VII.]

### 12.4 The phallic function as the function of loss

Fink defines the phallic function as the function that institutes lack, identifying it with the alienating operation of language. This is an explanatory compression. It helps show why the formulas concern more than genital sexuality, but it should not be treated as if Lacan always used “phallic function” as a simple synonym for language itself. The precise Lacanian formulations vary by period and context. [Source: Fink, *The Lacanian Subject*, Ch.8; comparison with Lacan, Seminar XX.]

The advantage of Fink's formulation is that it makes the universality of castration intelligible. If phallic function names a structural limit tied to signification, both anatomically male and female subjects are implicated. The later distinction between masculine and feminine positions therefore cannot be a distinction between castrated and uncastrated bodies.

### 12.5 Fink on the sexual non-relation

Fink's discussion of *rapport* is one of the most practically useful in the corpus. He notes that the French phrase can misleadingly sound as if Lacan were saying people do not have sexual intercourse. He interprets *rapport* in the more formal senses of relation, proportion, ratio, or definable relationship. Lacan's thesis is then that there is no direct formula that relates “man” and “woman” as reciprocal complementary terms. [Source: Fink, *The Lacanian Subject*, Ch.8, “There's No Such Thing as a Sexual Relationship.”]

Fink illustrates the missing relation by imagining the sort of equation that would make two terms add up to a whole. Such a relation would allow masculinity and femininity to be written as inverse or complementary functions. Lacan's theory denies that this is possible. The sexes are defined separately in relation to the phallic function, and their libidinal partners are not symmetrical. [Source: Fink, Ch.8.]

The value of this explanation is its clarity. Its risk is that the phrase “men and women do not interact directly” can sound like a psychological statement about interpersonal mediation. File 42 therefore retains the stronger primary emphasis on non-inscription: the missing relation is a failure of a formal complementary writing, not merely the observation that fantasy mediates relationships.

### 12.6 Fink on the formulas of sexuation

Fink devotes substantial effort to the four upper formulas and to the lower diagram. His account stresses that the masculine side is organized by the universal plus exception, whereas the feminine side has no exception and is not-all under the phallic function. This is broadly consistent with Seminar XX and is useful for English-language readers who find the compact matheme opaque. [Source: Fink, *The Lacanian Subject*, Ch.8, “The Formulas of Sexuation.”]

He also emphasizes that the sexuation formulas should not be confused with biological classification. Biological sex, social definitions of masculinity/femininity, and object choice can diverge. Lacan is trying to define psychoanalytic positions through a relation to language and jouissance. This is one of the strongest safeguards against crude application of the formulas to identity categories. [Source: Fink, Ch.8, “Distinguishing between the Sexes.”]

At the same time, Fink's language occasionally systematizes Lacan more cleanly than Lacan systematizes himself. The primary Seminar XX is more elliptical, performs its own logical difficulties, and places the formulas amid discussions of writing, love, Aristotle, mystical jouissance, knowledge, and the Other. A corpus synthesis should use Fink's clarity without mistaking it for the full texture of Lacan's argument.

### 12.7 Masculine position in Fink's reconstruction

Fink describes the masculine side as wholly subject to the phallic function, with the universal grounded by an exception. The lower masculine relation is organized through fantasy: the barred subject seeks object *a* as the cause of desire. This helps explain why a male-positioned subject's relation to a partner is structurally partial. The partner is not encountered as a complete Woman but through the object and the fantasy that stages desire. [Sources: Fink, Ch.8; Lacan, Seminar XX, formula table.]

This should not be converted into the empirical claim that men only objectify partners or that men are incapable of love. The schema concerns the logic of desire and jouissance, not a moral psychology of masculinity. Individuals can love, identify, care, and form complex bonds while still being subject to the structural mediation Lacan formalizes.

### 12.8 Feminine position in Fink's reconstruction

Fink's account of the feminine side emphasizes the absence of a constitutive exception and the consequent failure of a universal Woman. A feminine subject can be related to the phallic function and to phallic jouissance, but is not wholly contained by it. The lower schema writes a relation toward Φ and another toward S(Ā), opening the problem of Other jouissance. [Source: Fink, Ch.8; Lacan, Seminar XX, Chs. VI-VII.]

The key advantage of this account is that it avoids defining femininity simply as the negative of masculinity. The feminine side is not “everything the masculine side is not.” It is another logical treatment of the phallic function, with another relation to totalization and jouissance.

### 12.9 Fink's terminology and its limits

Fink sometimes translates and expands Lacan in a way designed for conceptual accessibility. This is useful for the corpus but requires provenance discipline. Expressions such as “signifierness of the signifier,” “jouissance packed in the signifier,” or the clean differentiation among biological sex, social gender, and sexuation are expository formulations. They may illuminate Lacan without being Lacan's own exact formulations in Seminar XX. [Source: Fink, *The Lacanian Subject*.]

The same is true of Fink's claim that the phallic function “institutes lack.” It captures a structural relation across Lacan's teaching but should not be turned into a citation-free definition of Φ. When exact logical notation is at issue, Seminar XX remains the source of record.

### 12.10 Fink and the relation between sexuation and discourse

Fink places sexuation within the wider Lacanian theory of the subject, fantasy, object *a*, and discourse. This prevents the sexual formulas from becoming an isolated “gender chapter.” Sexuation belongs to a system in which the subject is split by language, desire is caused by an object that cannot be assimilated to a normal object, knowledge is structurally positioned, and jouissance returns as remainder. [Source: Fink, *The Lacanian Subject*, Parts II-III and Ch.8.]

This systemic location is useful but must not be mistaken for a closed Lacanian system. Lacan's concepts shift over time, and Fink's book is explicitly a reconstruction that organizes those shifts into a teachable architecture.

### 12.11 The value of Fink for File 42

Fink's contribution can be summarized as three controls on interpretation. First, the phallus is a signifier, not an organ. Second, the sexual non-relation is a failure of formal complementarity, not a denial of sexual activity. Third, sexuation is a structural relation to the phallic function and jouissance, not a direct identity classification. These controls are retained throughout File 42. [Source: Fink, *The Lacanian Subject*, Ch.8.]

Where Fink goes beyond those controls into a systematic interpretation of what Lacan “really means,” File 42 rechecks the primary sources and labels the interpretation as Fink's. This preserves his considerable explanatory value without collapsing the distinction between commentator and primary text.


## 13. Alenka Zupančič's post-Lacanian development

### 13.1 Status of the source

Alenka Zupančič's *What Is Sex?* is not a commentary that merely translates Lacan into simpler terms. It is a philosophical intervention that takes Freud and Lacan as resources for rethinking sexuality, contradiction, ontology, and the subject. It must therefore be read in two registers: where Zupančič explicates a Lacanian claim, and where she extends it into her own theoretical construction. [Source: Zupančič, *What Is Sex?*, Introduction and Chs.1-3.]

This distinction is especially important in File 42 because many striking formulations associated with contemporary Lacanian theory of sex are Zupančič's philosophical elaborations. They should not be retroactively attributed to Lacan as if they appeared in Seminar XX in the same form.

### 13.2 Sex is not a positive substance

Zupančič rejects the idea that sexuality is a positive entity whose properties could simply be listed. Her starting point is psychoanalysis's discovery that sexuality is constitutively displaced, partial, and attached to activities that cannot be neatly separated into “sexual” and “nonsexual.” The sexual is not a specialized domain added to an otherwise neutral human being. It cuts across speech, enjoyment, fantasy, and drive. [Source: Zupančič, *What Is Sex?*, Introduction and Ch.1.]

This is consistent with Freud's discovery of polymorphous sexuality and with Lacan's insistence that jouissance is not confined to genital intercourse. Zupančič's distinctive step is to frame this as a philosophical problem of negativity rather than as a problem of identifying a hidden sexual content behind apparently nonsexual acts.

### 13.3 Non-relation as constitutive, not accidental

In “The Quandary of the Relation,” Zupančič treats Lacan's non-relation as more than the failure of two positive sexes to fit together. The non-relation is constitutive of the symbolic field. It is not an accident supervening on a relation that should otherwise work. [Source: Zupančič, *What Is Sex?*, Ch.2.]

This changes the usual explanatory direction. It is not that social conventions, neuroses, or bad communication disrupt a natural sexual harmony. Rather, fantasies, norms, identities, and sexual scenarios develop around an impossibility that has no natural harmonious baseline. What looks like disorder from the standpoint of an imagined relation is the ordinary organization of a field in which the relation is absent.

### 13.4 Sexuality as the problem produced by the non-relation

Zupančič makes a strong claim: the messiness of sexuality is not the cause of the absence of sexual relation; it is a consequence or manifestation of that absence. If there were a natural symbolic relation that prescribed complementary positions, sexuality could be reduced to execution of that relation. Instead, sexuality proliferates around the missing formula. [Source: Zupančič, *What Is Sex?*, Chs.2-3.]

This is a useful clarification of Lacan's point but is also a philosophical reformulation. Seminar XX itself works through jouissance, writing, Aristotle, love, mysticism, knowledge, and logic rather than presenting a single systematic thesis called “sexuality as the product of non-relation.” The corpus therefore attributes that condensation to Zupančič.

### 13.5 Sexual division as contradiction

The section “Sexual Division, a Problem in Ontology” is central to Zupančič's contribution. She argues that sexual difference should not be conceived as the difference between two positive kinds of being. Instead, division marks a contradiction or inconsistency internal to the symbolic/ontological field. [Source: Zupančič, *What Is Sex?*, Ch.3, “Sexual Division, a Problem in Ontology.”]

This is where her reading of the formulas of sexuation becomes especially important. The two sides are not two complete ontological substances called Man and Woman. They are two ways of formalizing or responding to the same constitutive impasse. Difference is therefore inseparable from contradiction.

### 13.6 The formulas as two configurations of one impossibility

Zupančič reads Lacan's formulas not as a set of features that distinguish two populations but as two logical configurations of the failure of symbolic totalization. The masculine side organizes contradiction through a universal founded on exception. The feminine side organizes it without the exception and therefore without closure into a universal whole. [Source: Zupančič, *What Is Sex?*, discussion of the formulas of sexuation in Ch.3.]

This reading converges with Soler's insistence that the feminine not-all is not an empirical minority statement. Zupančič, however, pushes the logic further toward ontology: each side is a different way in which the signifying order encounters its own constitutive impossibility.

### 13.7 Why this is not “two identities”

If sexuation were a theory of two identities, the task would be to determine which traits belong to each side and how persons identify with them. Zupančič's contradiction model reverses the problem. The division comes first as an impossibility of totalization; positive identities are secondary ways of organizing, narrating, or covering that impossibility. [Source: Zupančič, *What Is Sex?*, Ch.3.]

This does not imply that social identities are unreal or politically irrelevant. It means that, within this theoretical framework, no identity can provide the missing ontological closure. A social category can be real in its consequences and still fail to write the sexual relation.

### 13.8 Sex and ontology

Zupančič is careful not simply to declare psychoanalysis a new ontology. She engages Lacan's resistance to ontology while arguing that psychoanalysis has consequences for ontology precisely because it identifies a negativity that ontology tends to exclude in order to speak of being as a coherent field. Sex, on her account, names that internal break. [Source: Zupančič, *What Is Sex?*, Ch.3.]

The point is subtle. She is not saying “sex is the ultimate substance.” She is saying that sex coincides with a failure, contradiction, or impasse in symbolic being. That is why sex cannot be made into one more positive property of an entity.

### 13.9 The Real as contradiction, not hidden substance

Zupančič's use of the Lacanian Real is correspondingly anti-substantialist. The Real is not a positive realm behind language where true sexual essence resides. It is encountered as the impossibility or contradiction generated within symbolization itself. [Source: Zupančič, *What Is Sex?*, Ch.3 and Ch.4.]

This is highly relevant to the interpretation of feminine jouissance. A simplistic reading might place Other jouissance in a hidden Real beyond language. Zupančič's framework warns against this spatial metaphor. The “beyond” is not another complete domain; it is bound to the inconsistency of the symbolic and to the failure of closure.

### 13.10 From non-relation to social antagonism

Zupančič also asks how non-relation functions outside the narrowly sexual field. She discusses social relations, capitalism, exploitation, and the way social formations can make productive use of constitutive non-relations. Her argument is not that sexual non-relation and class antagonism are identical. Rather, the formal idea of non-relation provides a way to think structures that cannot be reconciled into a harmonious whole. [Source: Zupančič, *What Is Sex?*, Ch.2.]

For File 42, this material is useful as a limit test. It shows that “non-relation” is not a synonym for failed intimacy. It is a formal concept of structural antagonism. Yet the sexual formula remains specific and should not be dissolved into a generic theory of every social conflict.

### 13.11 The relation of sex to knowledge

Zupančič's approach also clarifies why the sexual non-relation cannot be repaired by discovering the right knowledge. If the problem were ignorance of a pre-existing relation, more knowledge could solve it. But if sex marks a contradiction internal to the symbolic, knowledge itself is implicated in the problem. [Source: Zupančič, *What Is Sex?*, Ch.3.]

This converges with Seminar XX's formal strategy. Lacan writes increasingly precise formulas, yet the result of the formalization is the writing of an impossibility. Knowledge reaches a point where it formalizes its own non-closure.

### 13.12 Zupančič on “sex or gender?”

Zupančič's section “Sex or Gender?” does not simply choose biological sex over social gender or vice versa. She questions the assumption that one can solve the sexual problem by allocating some features to nature and others to social construction. Psychoanalysis introduces a third dimension: a constitutive negativity that is neither a positive biological substance nor merely a freely constructible social identity. [Source: Zupančič, *What Is Sex?*, Ch.3, “Sex or Gender?”]

This is one reason her work is relevant to contemporary discussions without providing a direct policy or identity taxonomy. She shows how the psychoanalytic concept of sex cuts across the nature/culture binary while refusing to become an essence.

### 13.13 Limits of Zupančič's development

Zupančič's philosophical vocabulary can make Lacan appear more ontologically systematic than Seminar XX itself. Terms such as contradiction, negativity, and ontology organize her project and are indispensable to understanding *What Is Sex?*, but they should not be inserted back into every Lacanian statement as if they were Lacan's own explicit definitions. [Source: Zupančič, *What Is Sex?*; comparison with Lacan, Seminar XX.]

Similarly, her account of social exploitation of non-relation belongs to her broader theoretical project. It should not be used as evidence that Lacan's `rapport sexuel` was originally a general social theory.

### 13.14 The value of Zupančič for File 42

Zupančič's strongest contribution is to prevent the formulas of sexuation from hardening into two positive gender essences. By reading the two sides as two configurations of a common contradiction, she makes the non-relation primary and identity secondary. This deepens the logic of Lacan's formulas while remaining a post-Lacanian development that must be attributed as such.


## 14. Interpretive disputes, translation issues, and limits

### 14.1 The translation of `rapport sexuel`

The phrase `il n'y a pas de rapport sexuel` is translated variously as “there is no sexual relationship” and “there is no sexual relation.” Neither English phrase is fully neutral. “Relationship” can sound interpersonal or romantic, encouraging the false idea that Lacan denies the existence of couples. “Relation” better preserves a formal sense but can sound abstract enough to conceal that French *rapport* also carries senses of ratio, proportion, and relation between terms. [Sources: Lacan, Seminar XX; Fink, *The Lacanian Subject*, Ch.8.]

The safest practice is therefore not to choose one English phrase and forget the French. File 42 uses “sexual non-relation” as a technical shorthand and periodically returns to *rapport* to remind the reader that the claim concerns the non-inscription of a complementary relation, not the nonexistence of sexual acts or intimate bonds.

### 14.2 “Not-all” versus “not every”

One of the most important translation and logical disputes concerns the feminine formula conventionally rendered “not-all.” Ordinary English encourages a statistical reading: “not all women satisfy Φ” could mean that some do and some do not. That is not the structure Lacan is after. The right side simultaneously denies a founding exception and refuses universal totalization. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Ch. VII.]

Fink criticizes readings that turn the issue into a simple all/some opposition, while Soler insists on the logical specificity of the *pas-tout*. Zupančič further interprets the formulas through contradiction and antinomic structures. These readings differ in emphasis but converge in rejecting an empirical population interpretation. [Sources: Fink, *The Lacanian Subject*, Ch.8; Soler, *What Lacan Said about Women*; Zupančič, *What Is Sex?*.]

### 14.3 Does the feminine side lie outside the phallic function?

A recurrent dispute concerns the word “beyond.” Seminar XX speaks of a jouissance beyond the phallus, and the lower table gives barred Woman a relation to S(Ā). It is tempting to spatialize this into two territories: phallic language on one side and a pure feminine outside on the other. The formulas do not support such a clean partition. The feminine side also relates to Φ, and the not-all is not the negation of all phallic inscription. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Chs. VI-VII.]

Soler is especially useful on this point: supplementary jouissance does not abolish phallic jouissance. A feminine subject may participate in phallic jouissance while not being wholly circumscribed by it. “Beyond” therefore names a limit of totalization, not a second self-sufficient universe. [Source: Soler, *What Lacan Said about Women*.]

### 14.4 Is Other jouissance specifically female?

Lacan's terminology strongly associates supplementary jouissance with the feminine position and with “woman's jouissance.” This historical language should not be erased. Yet the logic of the formulas is not a biological ownership rule. The text does not warrant the simple empirical claim that every woman experiences Other jouissance or that every man is absolutely incapable of any relation to what the term names. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Ch. VI; Soler, *What Lacan Said about Women*.]

Different Lacanian schools place different emphasis on how open the feminine position is to subjects of either anatomy. File 42 does not adjudicate that dispute beyond what the sources support. It records that sexuation is not reducible to anatomy and that the formulas are logical positions, while preserving Lacan's own gendered nomenclature.

### 14.5 “The Woman does not exist” and misogynistic misreadings

The sentence is easily heard as a denial of women. In Lacan's logic, however, the barred Woman means that there is no universal signifier that would constitute Woman as a closed essence. The claim is therefore not that individual women are unreal, insignificant, or lacking humanity. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Chs. VI-VII.]

This does not immunize Lacan from feminist criticism. Critics can reasonably ask whether the theory's dependence on the phallus, its use of masculine/feminine labels, and its descriptions of women reproduce historical asymmetries. The corpus need not resolve those debates by declaration. It should instead keep clear what the formal claim is before evaluating its implications.

### 14.6 Phallocentrism and the status of the phallus

One line of criticism treats Lacan's privileged phallus as evidence that his theory makes male anatomy the norm. Lacan's defense would be that the phallus is a signifier, not the penis, and that the feminine not-all precisely prevents phallic function from exhausting sexuation. Fink strongly emphasizes this distinction. [Sources: Lacan, *Écrits*, “The Signification of the Phallus”; Fink, *The Lacanian Subject*, Ch.8.]

The counterargument is that symbolic privilege can remain gendered even when detached from anatomy. The fact that a signifier is not identical with an organ does not automatically settle the historical and political question of why that signifier is named phallus or what asymmetries follow from it. The project sources document the conceptual distinction more fully than they settle the broader feminist debate. File 42 therefore marks the issue rather than declaring it resolved.

### 14.7 Biological sex, psychoanalytic sex, and contemporary gender language

Lacan's theory contests the sufficiency of biology, but it does not contain today's full vocabulary of gender identity. Reading it as a direct anticipation of contemporary gender theory would be anachronistic. Conversely, dismissing it as merely a theory of anatomical male and female ignores the explicit effort to define sexuated positions through signification and jouissance. [Sources: Seminar XX; Fink; Soler.]

A disciplined use of the theory should therefore avoid both appropriation and dismissal. It can illuminate the non-coincidence of anatomy, identity, object choice, and mode of jouissance, but it does not supply a ready-made classification system for contemporary identities.

### 14.8 The status of logic in the formulas

Another dispute concerns how literally the formulas should be read as formal logic. Lacan borrows quantifiers and function notation, but his use is not simply a textbook application of classical predicate logic. The formulas carry psychoanalytic meanings concerning exception, castration, universality, and jouissance that exceed their minimal logical syntax. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Ch. VII.]

Fink reconstructs the logical relations pedagogically, while Zupančič reads them in relation to contradiction and the antinomies of reason. The existence of divergent sophisticated readings is itself evidence against treating the table as a four-line proposition whose meaning is obvious once the symbols are transliterated.

### 14.9 The mystical examples

Lacan's appeal to mystics can be read in several ways: as phenomenological testimony, as rhetorical provocation, as a way of naming unsayable enjoyment, or as an attempt to find textual traces of supplementary jouissance. The source does not justify turning mystical experience into a diagnostic marker of feminine sexuation. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX, Ch. VI.]

Nor should mystical references be taken as proof that Other jouissance is supernatural. The conceptual function of the examples is to indicate an experience that is testified to but not fully articulated in knowledge.

### 14.10 Is the sexual non-relation pessimistic?

A common reception treats the formula as a bleak statement that intimacy is doomed. Nothing in the logic requires that conclusion. The formula denies a necessary complementary inscription. It does not deny contingent bonds, sexual satisfaction, love, care, or durable relationships. Indeed, Seminar XX devotes sustained attention to love precisely within the field opened by non-relation. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX.]

The absence of a guarantee can be experienced as conflict, anxiety, or dissatisfaction, but it can also be the condition under which singular bonds are invented rather than deduced from a universal script. This last formulation is an inference from the Lacanian structure, not a direct promise made by Lacan.

### 14.11 Does love solve the non-relation?

No source in the approved set warrants the simple formula “love solves the sexual non-relation.” Love can make a bond, supplement an absence, or contingently write something where no necessary relation exists. But if love converted the non-relation into a universal complementarity, it would negate Lacan's thesis. [Source: Lacan, Seminar XX; Soler; Fink.]

The strongest defensible formulation is that love is one way speaking beings respond to or work within the absence of the sexual relation. The exact terminology varies among commentators and should be attributed.

### 14.12 Does psychoanalysis produce sexual harmony?

The theory gives no basis for promising that analysis will reveal the correct complementary partner or produce a state in which sexual difference becomes transparent. Analysis may alter a subject's relation to fantasy, demand, object *a*, symptom, love, and jouissance. It may make certain repetitive impasses more legible. But the structural non-relation is not a symptom to be cured. [Sources: Seminar XX; broader Lacanian clinical framework.]

This is a significant limit on therapeutic appropriation. The concept should not be used to normalize suffering by saying “relationships never work,” nor to promise transcendence of the non-relation through analysis.

### 14.13 Primary Lacan versus Soler, Fink, and Zupančič

The three principal interpreters in File 42 do different jobs. Fink builds a systematic explanatory bridge. Soler develops a distinctly Lacanian clinical and conceptual account of women, femininity, love, and jouissance. Zupančič develops the non-relation into a philosophical theory of contradiction and ontology. Their agreements should not erase these differences. [Sources: Fink, *The Lacanian Subject*; Soler, *What Lacan Said about Women*; Zupančič, *What Is Sex?*.]

Where all three converge, the convergence is evidence of a strong Lacanian interpretive tradition, not proof that every formulation appears in Lacan. Where they diverge, File 42 preserves the divergence rather than manufacturing a synthetic “Lacanian theory says” voice.

### 14.14 Limits of the source corpus

The present corpus is strong on Seminar XX, Fink, Soler, and Zupančič, but it is not exhaustive of the international debate on sexuation. It does not include the complete French seminar apparatus, all Millerian developments, feminist critiques across traditions, queer-theoretical appropriations, or every later Lacanian school's revision of the formulas. The file therefore aims at a rigorous project-internal synthesis, not a universal state-of-the-field review.

This limitation is methodologically useful. It prevents apparent comprehensiveness from being purchased by unsourced generalization. Claims outside the approved source set are excluded rather than silently supplied from model knowledge.

### 14.15 The risk of turning logic into stereotype
